Figure 24.5a - Case 2A board (Screens 11A to 11C): primary box capture with the work-in-progress summary, secondary carton scanning with the per-SKU expected, scanned and balance table, and the carton-wise variance report.
{Heading2} 24.6 Amendments from Session 06
Case 2A was re-presented at the start of Session 06 in higher fidelity and the screen numbering was confirmed on the board as Screens 11A, 11B and 11C. The numbering used earlier in this document has been corrected accordingly.
The walkthrough also settled how counting behaves where a supplier does not print a unique code on every unit. In many consignments every unit of a part carries the same code, so scanning the same code repeatedly is the means of counting units rather than an error. Some suppliers do differentiate, adding an extra character or a distinct code to each individual part, but the system cannot rely on it.
Note: this subsection records only what Session 06 added to or changed in Case 2A. The screens, fields and base behaviour remain as set out in sections 24.1 to 24.5.
FR-021.19: The screens for Case 2A shall be numbered 11A, 11B and 11C as confirmed on the Session 06 board. The earlier designations 12a, 12b and 12c are withdrawn, and Screens 12a and 12b are reassigned to the Binning module as set out in section 29.
FR-021.20: Where the same product code is scanned more than once within a primary carton, each scan shall increment the quantity counted for that product. Repeated scanning of an identical code shall be treated as the means of counting units, not as a duplicate error.
FR-021.21: The system shall accommodate both supplier conventions: a single shared code across all units of a part, and a distinct code carrying an additional character for each individual unit. Counting shall not depend on the supplier providing unique per-unit codes.
FR-021.22: The count for a product shall also be enterable manually, so that a quantity can be recorded where scanning is impractical or where the operator is correcting a scanned count.
FR-021.23: Condition shall be capturable per part at the point the part is selected, and a photograph shall be capturable against each part inspected.
FR-021.24: The variance shall express the difference between expected and found units at product level - for example ten units expected against nine found shall be reported as a variance of one unit short.
FR-021.25: The consignment check summary shall be reportable at SKU level, stating how many units of each product were found across the consignment, and shall be printable and exportable.
 [IMG]
Figure 24.6a - Case 2A re-presented in Session 06 with the screen numbering shown on the board: Screen 11A primary box capture and work-in-progress summary, Screen 11B secondary carton scanning with the per-SKU expected, scanned and balance table, and Screen 11C the carton-wise variance report.
{Heading1} 25. FR-022: Case 2B - Secondary Cartons Inside the Primary Cartons, Primary-Secondary Linkage Not Available
{Heading2} 25.1 Overview
Case 2B covers the arrangement in which secondary cartons sit inside the primary carton and both are scannable, but the primary-secondary linkage is not available. The supplier has uploaded the list of primary cartons and the list of secondary packs, but not the mapping that says which pack belongs in which carton.
Linkage availability follows ship list availability. Where a ship list has been provided the linkage exists; where it has not, it does not. That both codes are scannable does not imply a linkage: the primary carton is coded for the supplier's own dispatch and logistics purposes, and the secondary pack is coded for product identification. The two codings are independent.
The linkage that the supplier did not provide is created by the physical check itself. The operator scans the primary carton, then scans each secondary pack found inside it, and the system stores that association. The primary carton is therefore still captured even though its expected contents are unknown - it is the only way to know which of the unloaded cartons have been checked and which have been missed, and it allows the warehouse to report back to the supplier which carton was found to hold which products. The supplier's packing department holds this data at their end even when it is not shared, so a discrepancy can be settled against it.
The approach is otherwise identical to Case 2A. The single difference is in reporting: because the expected contents of each carton are unknown, no primary-carton-wise variance can be produced.
Note: the Case 2B board is laid out as a six-step flow rather than as numbered screens - Select Consignment &amp; Start, Scan Primary Carton, Scan Secondary Cartons, Decide Action, Finish Consignment Check, and Consignment Check Summary. The step numbers below follow the board.
{Heading2} Layman Description
The same physical situation as the previous row - small boxes inside a big carton, everything scannable - except that the supplier has not told you which belongs to which. Without that information the system cannot say a box is missing from a particular carton; it can only work with totals. What a good system does here is build the relationship as the work happens: as the operator opens carton A and scans the boxes inside it, the system records that those boxes came out of that carton. By the end of the check you have the linkage the supplier never gave you. Two things follow from that. Future consignments can be checked more precisely, and the record can be sent back to the supplier so his own packing information improves.
Example: Carton B is opened and 5 boxes are scanned out of it. The system had no advance information but now records those 5 against carton B. The same is done for the other 9 cartons. At the end the storekeeper has a complete carton-by-box map that did not exist that morning, and it is sent to the supplier - who from the next consignment starts providing it himself.
{Heading2} 25.2 Functional Requirements
FR-022.1: Case 2B shall be applied where secondary cartons are contained inside the primary carton, both primary and secondary cartons are scannable, supplier details have been uploaded, and the primary-secondary linkage is not available.
FR-022.2: The system shall treat the availability of the ship list as determining the availability of linkage. Where a ship list has been provided the linkage shall be taken to be available; where it has not, the linkage shall be taken to be unavailable.
FR-022.3: The system shall not infer a linkage from the fact that both codes are scannable. The primary carton is coded for the supplier's dispatch and logistics purposes and the secondary pack for product identification, and the two are independent.
FR-022.4: The approach shall otherwise be the same as Case 2A, and the behaviour specified for Case 2A shall apply except where this section states otherwise.
FR-022.5: Step 1 shall display the consignment details together with the upload status received from the supplier, listing which of the primary box list, secondary packs list and product list have been uploaded, and stating explicitly that the primary-to-product linkage is not available.
FR-022.6: Step 2 shall capture the active primary carton by scan and shall record the carton number, the time at which it was opened, the user who opened it and its status.
FR-022.7: On opening a primary carton the system shall state that linkage is not available and shall instruct the operator to scan all secondary cartons inside that primary carton.
FR-022.8: The system shall establish the primary-to-secondary association at the point of scanning. Scanning the primary carton and then the secondary products found within it shall create and store the linkage that the supplier did not provide.
FR-022.9: The primary carton shall continue to be captured even though its expected contents are unknown, so that the system can establish which of the unloaded cartons have been checked and which have been missed.
FR-022.10: The association established during the check shall be reportable back to the supplier, stating which primary carton was found to contain which products and in what quantities.
FR-022.11: Steps 2 and 3 shall maintain a work-in-progress count for the active primary carton showing secondary packs scanned, unique products scanned and total units scanned. These shall be actual scanned counts only, since no expected quantity exists for the carton.
FR-022.12: Step 3 shall list every product scanned in the active primary carton with product code, description and quantity in units, and shall provide a search alternative for codes that cannot be read.
FR-022.13: Step 4 shall capture product condition against the standard condition set and shall capture two additional checks - whether insurance is required and whether the product is eligible for issue for usage - each answered yes or no.
FR-022.14: Step 4 shall offer four next actions: Proceed to BIN, Bin Later, Issue for Usage for immediate workshop use, and Scan Next Secondary Carton to continue within the same primary carton.
FR-022.15: Step 5 shall state that all primary cartons have been scanned and shall request confirmation before the consignment check is closed, and shall offer Check Product Status and Refresh Consignment Status.
FR-022.16: An audit trail shall be captured for all scans, conditions and actions once the consignment check is finished.
FR-022.17: Step 6 shall present a summary of actual scanned data covering primary cartons expected, primary cartons checked, secondary packs scanned, unique products scanned and total units scanned.
FR-022.18: The summary shall report actual quantity at SKU level across the consignment and shall report quality issues across the consignment by qualitative aspect - damaged, packing tampered, visible scratch and wrong delivery.
FR-022.19: Primary-carton-wise variance shall not be produced in this case, because the expected contents of each carton are unknown. This is the only difference from Case 2A.
FR-022.20: Where the supplier has uploaded expected product quantities at gate entry level, variance may be reported at consignment level only, on request. Where no expected quantity has been uploaded, variance shall not be applicable.
FR-022.21: The consignment check summary shall be exportable as a report.
{Heading2} 25.3 Screen Field Reference - Steps 1 &amp; 2 (Consignment Check and Scan Primary Carton)
Note: the upload status block on Step 1 is what selects this case. It is the only place in the flow where the operator is told, before any scanning begins, that the linkage will have to be built by hand.
[TABLE]
| Field | Control Type | Description |
| Gate Entry Number (GEN), GEN Date | Read-only fields | Identify the consignment being checked and the date it entered the warehouse. |
| Supplier, Supplier Category | Read-only fields | Supplier name and category, for example OEM, carried from gate entry. |
| Document Type, Document No. | Read-only fields | The shipment document under which the consignment arrived, for example a tax invoice. |

{Heading1} 26. FR-023: Case 2C - Secondary Cartons Inside the Primary Cartons, Only the Secondary Product Scannable
{Heading2} 26.1 Overview
Case 2C covers the arrangement in which secondary cartons sit inside the primary carton, only the secondary product is scannable, and the primary-secondary linkage is not available. The supplier has uploaded the secondary product details but the primary carton carries no readable code.
Because the primary carton cannot be scanned, the operator is offered a choice at the start of each carton: print a box reference barcode sticker and stick it on the carton, in which case the flow proceeds exactly as Case 2B, or continue without a sticker and go straight to scanning the products inside. Where no sticker is used the system cannot identify individual cartons and instead counts how many primary cartons have been opened for checking.
From that point the flow is the same as Case 2B. Each product inside the carton is scanned, its condition recorded, and it is sent to binning, deferred, or issued for usage. On completion the consignment check is finished and the same summary is produced, again without primary-carton-wise variance.
Note: the opened cartons counter only advances when the operator confirms that the current carton is finished and moves to the next one. It is a count of cartons opened, not an identification of them, which is why no carton-level reporting is possible on this path.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Here the big carton has nothing to scan but the items inside it do. The difficulty is that if the outer carton has no identity, there is no way to say which carton something came out of, and no way to report a problem against a specific carton. There are two ways round it. Either print a sticker and give the carton an identity on the spot, which is the better answer because everything afterwards can refer to it, or simply keep a count of how many cartons have been opened, which is weaker but faster. Which of the two a system offers is worth establishing precisely, because both would be described as handling it, and only one leaves you able to point at a particular carton afterwards.
Example: Ten unmarked cartons arrive containing parts that are individually barcoded. The operator prints a sticker for each carton as he opens it, so carton 3 becomes a real reference. He scans 12 parts out of carton 3 and one is damaged. The record says part X, damaged, from carton 3. Where only a counter is used, the same record would say damaged, from one of ten cartons - which is much harder to take to a supplier.
{Heading2} 26.2 Functional Requirements
FR-023.1: Case 2C shall be applied where secondary cartons are contained inside the primary carton, only the secondary product is scannable, the secondary product details have been uploaded by the supplier, and the primary-secondary linkage is not available.
FR-023.2: Step 1 shall display the consignment details and the supplier upload status, showing the primary box list as not available, the secondary product list and product details as uploaded, and the primary-to-product linkage as not available.
FR-023.3: Step 2 shall state that the primary carton code is not scannable and shall offer two options: print a box reference barcode sticker and affix it to the primary carton, or continue without printing a sticker.
FR-023.4: Where the operator chooses to print a box reference barcode, the flow shall thereafter behave as Case 2B with a sticker, because the carton has become scannable.
FR-023.5: Where the operator continues without a sticker, the system shall count how many primary cartons have been opened for checking rather than identifying each carton individually.
FR-023.6: The opened cartons counter shall increment only when the operator confirms that the current carton is complete and moves to the next carton.
FR-023.7: Step 3 shall capture each secondary product inside the open carton by scanning its barcode, and shall provide a search alternative for codes that cannot be read.
FR-023.8: Step 3 shall maintain a list of products scanned in the current carton showing product code, description and quantity.
FR-023.9: Step 4 shall capture product condition against the standard condition set, defaulting to OK, together with the insurance required and issue for usage eligibility checks.
FR-023.10: Step 4 shall offer the next actions Proceed to BIN, Bin Later and Issue for Usage, and shall allow the work-in-progress counts for the carton to be refreshed.
FR-023.11: Step 5 shall state that all secondary products have been scanned for all primary cartons and shall request confirmation before the consignment check is closed.
FR-023.12: Step 6 shall present a summary of actual scanned data covering primary cartons opened, secondary products scanned, unique products scanned and total units scanned.
FR-023.13: The summary shall report actual quantity at SKU level across the consignment and quality issues across the consignment, and shall be exportable as a report.
FR-023.14: Primary-carton-wise variance shall not be produced in this case, for the same reason as Case 2B and additionally because individual cartons are not identified.
{Heading2} 26.3 Screen Field Reference - Steps 1 &amp; 2 (Start Consignment Check and Primary Carton)
Note: Step 2 is the branch point for this case. Printing a sticker converts the carton into an identifiable one and the flow becomes Case 2B; continuing without one means cartons can only be counted.
[TABLE]
| Field | Control Type | Description |
| Consignment Details | Read-only panel | Gate entry code and date, supplier, document type and number, and the number of invoices in the consignment. |
| Upload Status (From Supplier) | Status list | Shows the primary box list as not available, the secondary product list and product details as uploaded, and the primary-to-product linkage as not available. |
| Only secondary products scannable notice | Information banner | States that only the secondary products can be scanned and that the primary carton code is not available. |
| Start Consignment Check | Primary action | Opens the check for this consignment. |
| Primary carton not scannable warning | Warning banner | States that the primary carton code cannot be scanned and that the operator may either print a box reference barcode sticker or continue without printing. |

| Cancel | Action | Abandons the current carton. |

 [IMG]
Figure 26.4a - Case 2C board: the six-step flow with the primary carton shown as not scannable, the print-sticker or continue-without choice at Step 2, secondary product scanning inside the carton, and the summary without carton-level variance.
{Heading1} 27. FR-024: Case 2D - Secondary Cartons Inside the Primary Cartons, Nothing Scannable and No Supplier Details - Screens 13A, 13B, 13C &amp; 13D
{Heading2} 27.1 Overview
Case 2D is the weakest data position in the whole inward flow. Secondary cartons sit inside the primary carton, nothing is scannable, no supplier details are available and no linkage exists. Neither the carton nor the products inside it carry a readable code, and the system holds no expectation of what should be there.
This is not an edge case. A number of the newer original equipment manufacturers do not barcode even their primary products - the tempo traveller segment was cited as an example - and their parts carry no MRP stickers. Dealers also stock items bought locally, such as distilled water, which are not serialised at all but are still held in inventory and must be receivable through the same system.
Identification is therefore manual. The operator either prints product barcode stickers so that products can be identified during inspection, or searches the product master by code, description or part number. Quantity is counted physically. Condition is captured for the selected product only, one item at a time, with damaged units set aside for insurance. Binning then proceeds against the recommended location held in the master, and the exception panel handles the no-location and next-product states.
Note: Screens 13A to 13D are numbered on the board. Their sequence is identify, review and act, bin, and handle the exception - the same shape as Cases 1C and 1D, but with the products discovered rather than expected.
{Heading2} Layman Description
The hardest case: nothing carries a barcode at all - not the outer carton, not the parts inside. Everything has to be identified by a person looking at it and finding it on a list. A system that handles this properly does three things. It switches the scanning controls off rather than leaving them on screen to be poked at pointlessly. It gives a fast way to find a part by typing a few letters of its name or number, since the alternative is scrolling through thousands of entries. And it still lets condition be recorded per part, because the goods are no less likely to be damaged for being unlabelled. This case is more common than people expect - loose items, local fabricators, anything repacked in transit - and a system that quietly assumes everything is scannable becomes useless the moment it happens.
Example: A local fabricator delivers 40 brackets loose in unmarked cartons. The scanner is greyed out. The operator types brac and the part comes up in the list. He records 40 received, 2 bent, and prints stickers so the cartons have references for the rest of their journey. The whole consignment is on record without a single barcode having been read.
{Heading2} 27.2 Functional Requirements
FR-024.1: Case 2D shall be applied where secondary cartons are contained inside the primary carton, nothing is scannable, no supplier details are available and the primary-secondary linkage is not available.
FR-024.2: The system shall support consignments from suppliers who do not barcode their primary products at all, and locally purchased non-serialised items which carry no scannable code but are held in stock.
FR-024.3: Screen 13A shall disable the primary box and product code scan controls and shall indicate on screen that they are inactive for this case.
FR-024.4: Screen 13A shall offer two identification methods: print product barcode stickers so that products can be identified manually during inspection, or search the product master by product code, description or part number.
FR-024.5: Where product barcode stickers are printed, the sticker shall carry the product code, description and pack size.
FR-024.6: Screen 13A shall maintain an opened cartons counter and shall list the products identified in the currently opened carton with product code, description and quantity.
FR-024.7: More than one product shall be capable of being added to the same opened carton, since the carton holds several secondary items.
FR-024.8: Screen 13B shall display the selected product with its code, description, manually entered quantity split between good and damaged units, pack size, binning zone, recommended binning location and alternate locations.
FR-024.9: Screen 13B shall list the other products already identified in the current opened carton alongside the selected product, so that the operator retains the context of the carton.
FR-024.10: Condition shall be captured for the selected product only. The system shall state that the condition recorded applies to the selected product and that other products will be captured when they are selected.
FR-024.11: Condition shall be captured item by item within the selected product, with a photograph capturable against each item and an outcome of Proceed to BIN or Set Aside for insurance.
FR-024.12: Screen 13B shall maintain counters for good units, damaged units and insurance claims, and shall offer Issue for Usage, Proceed to BIN, BIN LATER, Continue Identifying Products and Cancel.
FR-024.13: Screen 13C shall bin the selected product in two verified steps - select or scan the recommended location, then confirm the product - and shall state how many units will be binned and how many set aside before Confirm BIN is available.
FR-024.14: Screen 13C shall offer Continue Identifying Next Product and Cancel Current Product so that the operator can move through the carton product by product.
FR-024.15: Screen 13D shall present the no-location alert state with Enter Location and Notify Admin, subject to role-based access control.
FR-024.16: Screen 13D shall present the next product and next opened carton state, confirming that progress has been saved and offering Identify Next Product Manually and Next Opened Carton.
FR-024.17: Screen 13D shall display the opened cartons counter, the number of products identified in the current carton and the bin later queue with its pending count.
{Heading2} 27.3 Screen Field Reference - Screen 13A (Identify Products)
Note: the two identification methods are offered side by side because they suit different situations - printing stickers is worthwhile where a carton holds many units of the same part, and searching is faster where it holds a few of several.

{Heading1} 28. FR-025: Open Delivery - Primary Carton Capture, Duplicate Scan Control and Bin Location Selection
{Heading2} 28.1 Overview
The open delivery flow introduced in section 21 was extended in this session with a primary carton capture step, controls around duplicate scanning, and an explicit bin location selection where a product may be stocked in more than one place.
The inspection itself is unchanged in principle. Pieces are examined one at a time; a piece damaged because the outer carton was damaged is photographed and recorded as damaged, and it is set aside for insurance while the remaining good pieces proceed to binning. What was added is the handling around it: how the carton is captured when its code cannot be read, what happens when a code that should be unique is scanned twice, and how the operator chooses between several permitted bin locations.
Two distinct code situations are now separated. Where a secondary code is genuinely unique to the unit, scanning it a second time is an error and is blocked. Where a code is not unique - the common case in which every unit of a part carries the same code - the system offers to create and print a unique scan code to be stuck on the secondary pack, so that the unit can be tracked individually.
Note: binning and issue for usage are the two destinations from this flow. Where a piece is routed to a workshop job rather than to stock it does not go to a bin at all, so the phrase proceed to binning should be read as proceed to the appropriate destination.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Duplicate Scan Control - Scanning the same item twice by accident is easy - the scanner beeps, the operator is not sure it registered, he scans again. If the code genuinely identifies that one physical unit, the second scan is a mistake and the system should block it, otherwise one item is counted as two and the stock figure inflates. But not every code is unique to a unit. Many suppliers print the same part number barcode on every box of that part, so ten identical boxes carry ten identical codes and scanning them ten times is entirely correct. A system that blocks all repeats will make those ten boxes impossible to receive. The good answer is to block genuine duplicates and, where codes are shared, offer to generate a separate code per unit.
Example: The operator scans a serial-numbered gearbox, is not sure it took, and scans again - the system blocks the second scan and says already scanned. Later he receives ten identical filter boxes all carrying the same part barcode. Here it does not block him; it offers to print a unique label for each so the ten boxes can be told apart afterwards.
Carton Capture - When a damaged carton is taken for opening, the system records which carton it was and when the examination started. That timestamp does more work than it appears to. Open delivery is usually done in the presence of the driver or the transporter, and the time it happened is what demonstrates the carton was opened on arrival rather than three days later - which is the first thing challenged when a claim is disputed. It also records where the supplier has not provided information linking the carton to its contents, so the reader knows whether the expected contents were independently known or simply what was found. The distinction matters to anyone relying on the record afterwards.
Example: Carton 31 comes off wet at 09:52 and is opened at 10:15 with the driver present. Both times are on the record. When the transporter later argues the carton was fine on delivery and must have got wet in the warehouse, the twenty-three minute gap between arrival and opening, with the driver signature, ends the discussion.
Evidence Capture - A photograph of the outside of a carton shows a crushed corner. It does not show that three of the twelve alternators inside are cracked. Being able to photograph each piece individually is what turns the inspection into evidence, because the claim is about the pieces, not the box. Each photograph attaches to the specific piece it belongs to rather than to the carton generally, so the record shows this unit, damaged, with this image. The other half is that pieces which pass go on to be binned normally from the same screen - the operator should not have to finish the inspection, leave, and start a separate putaway job for the nine good ones.
Example: Three of twelve alternators are cracked. The operator photographs each of the three separately as he works through them. The claim goes to the supplier with three images, each tied to a piece. The remaining nine are sent to bin from the same screen and are on the shelf ten minutes later - no second job, no re-scanning.
{Heading2} 28.2 Functional Requirements
FR-025.1: The open delivery flow shall begin with capture of the primary carton, showing the gate entry code, supplier and an optional document number.
FR-025.2: The system shall state where the primary-secondary linkage is not available and shall instruct the operator to capture the primary carton first and then scan the secondary product code.
FR-025.3: The primary box shall be capturable by scan or selection, and where the barcode or QR code is smudged the operator shall be able to enter the box details manually or select the box using predictive search.
FR-025.4: On capture the system shall record and display the box reference, the time at which it was captured and the user who captured it.
FR-025.5: The captured carton screen shall provide navigation to the open delivery queue, described as the cartons marked for open delivery during unloading.
FR-025.6: The inspection screen shall show the selected primary box together with the observation recorded at unloading and the open delivery marked flag.
FR-025.7: Each piece shall be inspected individually, with the selected piece presented separately and its condition recorded against the standard condition set.
FR-025.8: A photograph shall be capturable against the selected piece, and the piece shall be markable for an insurance claim.
FR-025.9: The system shall state that a piece found to be in acceptable condition proceeds to binning and that a piece found otherwise is kept aside.
FR-025.10: Where more than one bin location exists for a product, the system shall present the permitted locations for selection rather than assuming one. Part-to-location combinations shall be uploaded by the administrator.
FR-025.11: Where a code that is unique to the unit is scanned a second time, the system shall block the scan and shall state that duplicate scanning is not allowed for unique SKU codes.
FR-025.12: Where a product code is not unique to the unit, the system shall offer to create and print a unique scan code to be affixed to the secondary pack so that individual units can be tracked.
FR-025.13: The level at which unique scan code generation is configured - by SKU, by product category or by supplier - shall be an administrative setting.
FR-025.14: Where no bin location is found for a product, the system shall raise a no-location alert offering two paths: manual entry of a location where the administrator permits it, and a request routed to the administrator to update the bin location for the product where they do not.
FR-025.15: Binning and issue for usage shall be treated as alternative destinations for an inspected piece. A piece routed to a workshop job shall not be binned.
{Heading2} 28.3 Screen Field Reference - Capture Primary Carton
Note: this screen is new to the open delivery path. It exists because the carton has to be identified before its contents can be attributed to it, and because a carton flagged as damaged at unloading may well have a damaged code.

 [IMG]
Figure 28.5a - Open Delivery extended: primary carton capture with smudged-code handling, the open delivery queue with unload observations carried forward, piece-by-piece inspection of the selected box, and bin location selection with the duplicate scan, unique scan code and no location alerts.
{Heading1} 29. FR-026: Binning - Cart Assembly - Screens 12a &amp; 12b
{Heading2} 29.1 Overview
Binning is the stage after the consignment check. By this point the primary and secondary cartons have been scanned, damaged products have been identified and set aside, and every remaining product carries a binning zone, a binning location and any alternate locations from master data.
Physical binning is done with a cart. The operator collects products into a cart or trolley, scanning each one as it goes in, and then takes the loaded cart to the bin locations. Scanning the same product repeatedly increases the quantity on that cart line; scanning a different product adds a new line. When the cart is physically full the operator proceeds to bin it and the next cart becomes active.
How much a cart can hold is a physical matter and is deliberately left outside the system. The operator judges it, taking account of fragility - a heavy item should not be stacked on top of something fragile - which is why the fragile flag is surfaced at the point of adding to the cart rather than only at binning.
One cart can serve more than one bin location. A cart assembled for a single row of the warehouse may have items destined for the start, the middle and the far end of that row. The cart details are therefore to be structured as cart, then location, then the parts and quantities going to that location, rather than as a flat list of parts.
Note: at cart assembly the location is entered rather than scanned. The operator is recording where each item is destined to go. The location scan happens later, at the bin itself, as the confirmation that the right place has been reached. Product scanning at cart assembly is appropriate because the item is physically being picked up and placed into the cart at that moment.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Cart Assembly (FR-026.1 to .9) - Rather than walking a single part to a shelf and coming back, the operator loads several parts onto a trolley and puts them all away in one trip. The system keeps a list of what is on each trolley, so the trolley itself becomes something that can be tracked - what is on it, where each item is going, and how much is left to do. Organising the list by trolley and then by location is what makes the trip efficient: the operator can see that four items on this trolley all go to the same aisle. Fragile items are flagged in the list so they are not stacked underneath something heavy, which is a decision made when loading rather than discovered at the shelf.
Example: The operator loads twelve parts onto trolley 2. The list shows them grouped by location: four for aisle B, five for aisle C, three for aisle D, with one headlamp marked fragile. He walks aisle B, then C, then D, in one loop rather than twelve separate trips, and the fragile item goes on top.
Cart Assembly (FR-026.21 to .23) - Three small things that decide whether cart assembly is quick or painful. First, the standard pack size appears on its own from the part record, so the operator can see that this item normally comes in boxes of five without looking it up. Second, quantity can be typed. Adding ten of the same part by scanning it ten times is the sort of thing that looks fine in a demonstration and is abandoned within a week on a real floor. Third, a line added by mistake can be deleted while the trolley is still being loaded. Without that, the operator either lives with a wrong line or cancels the whole trolley and starts again, and both of those end with somebody adjusting stock later.
Example: The operator adds a filter to the trolley and the screen shows a standard pack of five. He is loading ten, so he types 10 rather than scanning ten times. He then adds a wrong part by scanning the carton next to it, notices immediately, and deletes the line. The trolley leaves the bay with exactly what is on it, and nothing needs correcting afterwards.
No-Location Handling - Sometimes a part has no storage location set up for it at all - a new item, or one that has never been stocked here before. The operator is holding it and has to do something. There are two reasonable answers and the difference between them is a policy decision, not a technical one. Either he is trusted to choose a sensible place himself, with the system recording what he chose, or he is stopped and the item waits until a supervisor defines a proper location. Which applies should depend on who he is, and that is what role-based control means here. Junior staff picking their own locations is how a warehouse ends up with stock nobody can find; senior staff being blocked is how a bay ends up jammed with parts nobody is allowed to move.
Example: A new part arrives with no location defined. A senior storekeeper is allowed to place it, chooses C-04-02, and the system records that he did so and that it was a manual choice. A junior operator with the same part is blocked, and the item goes into a pending list for the supervisor. Both outcomes are deliberate, and neither leaves the part standing on the floor unrecorded.
Expected Quantity Control - An administrator can switch off the display of how many pieces are expected, so the operator has to count what is physically there and enter that number, rather than seeing a figure and confirming it. It exists because of a well-known human problem. If the screen says twelve, a tired man at the end of a shift will type twelve without counting, and eleven pieces will be recorded as twelve. Hiding the expected number forces a genuine count, and the difference between what he enters and what was expected becomes real information rather than a number copied off a screen. It is not always wanted - it slows the work - so it is an option an administrator turns on where accuracy matters more than speed.
Example: For a high-value line the administrator hides expected quantities. The operator opens a carton that should hold twelve alternators, counts eleven, and enters 11. The system flags one short. Had the screen shown twelve with a confirm button, the shortage would almost certainly have been confirmed away and found weeks later at a stock count.
{Heading2} 29.2 Functional Requirements
FR-026.1: The system shall support cart-based binning, in which products are collected into a cart, scanned as they are added, and then taken to their bin locations for placement.
FR-026.2: Screen 12a shall capture the consignment details and shall provide a product code scan with a delete control, a quantity stepper, the pack size, the product description, the binning sub-zone and the binning location.
FR-026.3: Products flagged as fragile in the master shall be indicated at the point of adding to the cart, so that the operator can judge how the cart is loaded and avoid stacking heavy items on fragile ones.
FR-026.4: Where the same product is scanned more than once into a cart, the quantity on that cart line shall increase. Where a different product is scanned, a new line shall be added to the cart.
FR-026.5: The active cart number shall be displayed while items are being added.
FR-026.6: The physical capacity of a cart shall not be managed by the system. The operator shall determine when a cart is full.
FR-026.7: When the operator proceeds to bin the active cart, the next cart shall become active for further collection.
FR-026.8: Screen 12b shall present the cart details for each cart, listing part number, quantity and location together with the part description.
FR-026.9: The cart details shall be structured hierarchically as cart, then bin location, then the parts and quantities destined for that location, because one cart may serve more than one bin location.
FR-026.10: At cart assembly the bin location shall be entered or confirmed rather than scanned. Location scanning shall take place at the bin during binning execution, as the confirmation that the correct place has been reached.
FR-026.11: Product scanning shall remain part of cart assembly, since the product is physically being picked and placed into the cart at that point.
FR-026.12: Where no location exists in the master for the part being added to the cart, the system shall raise a no-location alert naming the part number and requesting that a location be added to the master.
FR-026.13: The binning screen shall allow the consignment to be identified by gate entry number, gate entry date, document type and document number.
{Heading2} 29.3 Screen Field Reference - Screen 12a (Binning - Add to Cart)
Note: the fragile flag appears on this screen rather than only at binning because the decision it informs - what goes on top of what - is taken while the cart is being loaded.
[TABLE]
| Field | Control Type | Description |
| Consignment Details | Collapsible section | The consignment against which the products being binned were received. |
| Scan Product Code | Scan or select field with delete | Captures each product as it is physically placed into the cart; the delete control removes an item added in error. |
| Quantity | Stepper control | Number of units of the scanned product being added to the cart; increases as the same product is scanned again. |
| MOQ | Read-only field | Quantity per pack size for the product, from the master. |

{Heading2} 29.5 Amendments from Session 07
The no-location alert is currently specified as raising a request for the master to be updated. The BRD ties the alert to role-based access control and provides for two outcomes rather than one. Where the administrator has permitted it, the user may bin the product to a location of their choice and continue. Where the administrator has not, the user is blocked and a message is routed to the administrator instead.
A second point was raised on the same screen. The BRD gives the administrator the option to hide the expected quantity to bin from the operator, so that the operator counts what is physically present rather than reading the expected figure off the screen and confirming it.
FR-026.14: The no-location alert shall be governed by role-based access control and shall resolve to one of two outcomes according to the permission held by the user.
FR-026.15: Where the administrator has permitted it, the user shall be able to bin the product to a location of their choice and continue.
FR-026.16: Where the administrator has not permitted it, the user shall be blocked from binning and a message shall be routed to the administrator instead.
FR-026.17: The administrator shall have the option to hide the expected quantity to bin from the operator, so that the quantity recorded is the quantity physically counted.
{Heading2} 29.6 Amendments from Session 07 (Part 2)
The administrator option to hide the expected quantity to bin, recorded at FR-026.17, was explained in full and the reasoning is worth preserving because it determines how the control must behave.
Where the expected quantity is displayed, an operator who is working at speed can pick a few units, declare the task finished and enter the expected figure - fifty, say - without having counted anything. The recorded quantity then matches the expectation exactly and nothing in the system indicates that a count did not take place.
Where the expected quantity is hidden, that shortcut is closed. The operator does not know what figure would satisfy the system, so the only way to complete the task is to scan each unit as it is placed. The count is built from the scans rather than asserted at the end.
Note: hiding the figure is what makes the scan meaningful, so the option should sit at the same configuration level as the manual entry control at FR-027.14. Hiding the expected quantity while permitting unrestricted manual entry would reopen the same gap by a different route.
FR-026.18: Where the expected quantity to bin is hidden, the quantity recorded shall be derived from the units actually scanned and shall not be enterable as a total.
FR-026.19: The system shall not indicate to the operator what quantity would satisfy the expectation while the expected quantity is hidden.
FR-026.20: The hide-expected-quantity setting and the manual entry permission at FR-027.14 shall be capable of being configured together, so that hiding the quantity is not defeated by unrestricted manual entry.
{Heading2} 29.7 Amendments from Session 07 (Part 4)
The cart assembly walkthrough settled three points left open earlier. The pack size displayed beside the quantity is drawn from the master data against the product code, not entered by the operator and not held against the consignment. It is shown for reference so the operator can see the standard pack in which the part is normally handled while deciding what to place in the cart.
Where a product cannot be scanned the operator enters the code, and where several units of the same product are being added the quantity is entered directly rather than by scanning the same item repeatedly. A delete control is available while the cart is being assembled, so that a line added in error can be removed before the cart proceeds to binning.
Note: this closes OI-072, which recorded the quantity entry point truncated at the end of Part 3. The behaviour described there is the behaviour specified here.
FR-026.21: The pack size displayed at cart assembly shall be retrieved from the product master against the product code. It shall be read-only and shall be displayed for reference only.
FR-026.22: Quantity shall be enterable directly on the cart line. The operator shall not be required to scan the same product once per unit in order to build up a quantity.
FR-026.23: A delete control shall be available against each cart line while the cart is being assembled, so that a line entered in error can be removed before the cart proceeds to binning.
{Heading1} 30. FR-027: Binning Execution - By Product, By Location and Direct - Screens 13a, 13b, 14a, 14b &amp; 15
{Heading2} 30.1 Overview
Once a cart is loaded it is taken to the bin. Three routes are provided. Binning by product starts from the product - the operator scans a product from the cart and the system states which location it is destined for. Binning by location starts from the location - the operator selects the location, goes to it, and the system states which product from the cart belongs there. Direct binning covers a single product placed without a cart at all.
All three end the same way. The operator scans the location and the system compares it against the location recorded on the cart line, raising a wrong location alert if they do not match. The product is then scanned as confirmation that the right part is being placed, with a wrong part alert if it is not. Quantity or number of packs is confirmed, and the placement is saved.
Placement need not be complete. Where there is not enough space at the bin, the operator may place some of the pieces and finish that item, and the remaining pieces are then moved to a different location chosen from the permitted list, scanned there, and updated.
Note: the wrong location and wrong part alerts are the reason the location is scanned here rather than at cart assembly. At the cart the operator states an intention; at the bin the system verifies that the intention was carried out.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Binning Routes - Three different ways of putting stock away, because three different situations exist. Working by part means taking one part and going to its location - sensible when there are a few items in scattered places. Working by location means going to a shelf and dealing with everything on the trolley destined for it - much faster when a trolley has several items for the same aisle. Direct binning is for a single part being put away on its own, with no trolley made up at all, which is what actually happens for the majority of small daily receipts. A system offering only one of the three forces the warehouse to work that way regardless, and the usual result is that operators stop using the trolley function and record things afterwards.
Example: A trolley with twelve parts across three aisles is worked by location - three stops instead of twelve. Later a single urgent filter arrives on its own; the operator uses direct binning and puts it away in under a minute without creating a trolley for one item. Same system, two entirely different jobs.
Scan Verification - The same two-scan discipline as elsewhere, applied at the moment of putting stock on the shelf: the location is scanned, the part is scanned, and only then is the quantity confirmed and the movement committed. What varies between systems is what happens when one of the scans is wrong. A system that simply refuses and gives no reason leaves the operator guessing whether he is at the wrong shelf or holding the wrong part - and after two or three of those he starts typing codes in to get past it. A system that says which of the two was wrong lets him correct it in seconds. It is worth asking to see a deliberate mistake made, because the quality of the error message is the whole difference here.
Example: The operator scans C-05-02 and then a filter. The screen says the part is expected at C-04-02, not here - he is one shelf out. He moves and scans again, and it is accepted. On a different occasion he is at the right shelf with a similar-looking part from the next bin, and the message names the part rather than the location. Two different mistakes, two different messages, both corrected on the spot.
Partial Placement - The intended shelf will not always hold everything being carried to it. Rather than forcing an all-or-nothing decision, the operator can put in what fits and record the remainder against a different location. Both parts of the movement are recorded, so the stock figures stay true to where the goods physically are. The alternative behaviours are both bad. If the system insists the whole quantity goes in one place, the operator either forces the stock in where it does not fit or carries it back to the bay and it sits there. If he can leave part of it unrecorded, the stock figure is wrong from that moment. Splitting properly is the only outcome that leaves the record matching the warehouse.
Example: The operator carries twenty filters to B-12-04 and only twelve will fit. He records 12 there, and the system asks where the remaining 8 are going. He walks to B-12-09 and records those. Stock shows 12 in one location and 8 in the other, which is exactly what a person walking the aisle would find.
Manual Entry Control - Barcodes get smudged, torn and rubbed off. When one will not scan, the operator has to be able to type the code in or the work stops. But typing is also how a system gets quietly bypassed - it is faster than scanning, so given a free choice some operators will type everything and the scanning discipline collapses without anyone noticing. The answer is to allow it under control: permitted by role, with a reason picked from a short list, and counted. The counting is the part supervisors care about. A report showing what proportion of movements were scanned rather than typed tells you whether the process is being followed, and a sudden rise in one shift or one operator is worth asking about.
Example: A carton arrives with the label rubbed away. The operator types the code and picks the reason label damaged from the list. That month the report shows 96 per cent scanned, 4 per cent typed - acceptable. The next month one operator shows 40 per cent typed, all with the reason scanner not working. His scanner is checked, found faulty, and replaced. Nobody would have known otherwise.
Location Sequencing - When working shelf by shelf, the operator needs to know where he is in the round: stop three of eight. Without it, a trolley with parts for eight locations becomes a job with no visible end, and he cannot tell a supervisor how long he will be. The second part is that when several items on the trolley go to the same shelf, the system presents the next one for that shelf before sending him anywhere else - so he deals with everything at that location in one visit rather than walking back later. The third is that if the shelf fills up partway through, he is told at that point rather than arriving with the next item and finding out then.
Example: Trolley 1 has parts for eight locations. At stop three the screen shows 3 of 8 and offers a second part for the same shelf, which he puts away without moving. Halfway through the third item that shelf is full, and the screen tells him immediately rather than after he has walked to stop four and back.
Quantity Reconciliation - Two numbers are kept rather than one: how many pieces were supposed to go into the location, and how many actually did. Where they differ, the difference is visible. Keeping only one number - usually by assuming the intended quantity was achieved - hides every short placement. The operator meant to put twelve in, only ten fitted, and the record says twelve, so the stock figure is two high from that moment and stays that way until a physical count finds it, by which time nobody can say when or where it happened. Two numbers cost nothing to record and mean a discrepancy is visible on the day it occurs, against a specific location and a specific person.
Example: The operator is meant to place twelve filters in B-12-04. Only ten go in; the other two are still on the trolley when he moves on. The record shows expected 12, actual 10, and the two are flagged as still to be placed. Without both figures, stock would show twelve in B-12-04 and a picker would find ten, three weeks later, with no explanation available.
{Heading2} 30.2 Functional Requirements
FR-027.1: The system shall provide three binning routes: binning by product, binning by location, and direct binning.
FR-027.2: Binning by product shall allow the operator to select a cart, scan a product from it, and be directed to the location recorded against that product.
FR-027.3: Binning by location shall allow the operator to select a cart, go to a location, and be shown the product from that cart which is destined for it.
FR-027.4: Direct binning shall allow a single product to be placed without a cart, capturing the product code, description, pack size, zone and location.
FR-027.5: In every route the location shall be scanned at the bin and confirmed against the location recorded for the item.
FR-027.6: Where the scanned location does not match the recorded location the system shall raise a wrong location alert and shall require the correct location to be scanned.
FR-027.7: The product shall be scanned at the bin as confirmation that the correct part is being placed.
FR-027.8: Where the scanned product does not match the expected product the system shall raise a wrong part alert and shall require the correct part to be scanned.
FR-027.9: The operator shall confirm the quantity or the number of packs actually placed before the transaction is saved.
FR-027.10: Partial placement shall be permitted. Where there is insufficient space at the bin, the operator shall be able to place only some of the pieces and complete that item.
FR-027.11: Pieces that could not be placed shall be capable of being assigned to a different location selected from the permitted list, scanned at that location and updated.
FR-027.12: Save shall record progress without closing the item, and Finish shall complete it.
FR-027.13: The bin locations associated with a cart shall be presentable as a list, so that the operator can see every location the cart must visit and select between them.
{Heading2} 30.3 Screen Field Reference - Screens 13a &amp; 13b (Binning by Product)
