{Heading2} 30.6 Amendments from Session 07
Binning execution is specified as a scan-based process throughout. Two controls around what happens when scanning is not possible were identified as missing.
The first is an administrative toggle. Where a product or location code is smudged or otherwise unscannable, whether the operator may key it manually is a decision for the administrator rather than for the operator. The concern is a practical one: an operator working at speed may scan a single product and then confirm the remainder as matched without actually checking them, and the manual path is where that shortcut becomes available.
The second is reporting on scan compliance. The warehouse needs to see what proportion of a load was actually scanned and what proportion was entered by hand - if ninety per cent scans and ten per cent does not, that ten per cent is where the risk sits. A reason should be captured against each manual entry, because the useful question is not that the operator could not scan but why: whether there was no sticker, or whether the nature of the product is such that it cannot carry one.
A photograph of the item that could not be scanned may also be captured. This was agreed as optional rather than required. The operator is already photographing products at condition capture, and the design intent throughout is to remove steps from the picker's work rather than add them, since a flow with too many steps invites mis-taps and slows the floor.
Note: the reason for manual entry is best offered as a short pick list rather than free text. The operator is working one-handed on a terminal, and a list of two or three causes will be answered where a text box will not.
FR-027.14: The administrator shall be able to define whether manual entry is permitted for smudged or unscannable product codes and location codes at binning.
FR-027.15: Where manual entry is permitted and used, the system shall capture a reason for the manual entry, distinguishing at minimum between the absence of a sticker and a product whose nature prevents scanning.
FR-027.16: The reason shall be captured through a short selection list rather than free text, so that it can be reported on and so that it does not slow the operator.
FR-027.17: The system shall report on products binned using scan and products binned without scan, expressed as a proportion of the load, so that scan compliance is visible.
FR-027.18: The operator may optionally capture a photograph of an item that could not be scanned. This shall not be mandatory.
FR-027.19: Additional steps in the binning flow shall be kept optional wherever possible. The flow shall remain as simple as possible for the operator, since unnecessary steps increase both handling time and the likelihood of a mis-selection.
{Heading2} 30.7 Amendments from Session 07 (Part 4)
Binning by location works through the locations a cart must visit in sequence rather than through its parts. The screen shows which location in that sequence the operator is at, and once the products destined for that location are placed it presents the next product queued for the same location before moving on. Where the next queued product cannot be placed because the location has filled, the operator is told so at the point of the attempt rather than on arrival at the next location.
The quantity fields distinguish what was expected from what was placed. Quantity to Bin is the figure carried from the cart; Actual Binned is what physically went into the location. The two are recorded separately so that a short placement is visible rather than absorbed.
FR-027.20: Binning by location shall display the position of the current location within the sequence of locations the selected cart must visit.
FR-027.21: Where more than one product from the cart is destined for the current location, the system shall present the next queued product for that location before directing the operator onwards.
FR-027.22: Where a queued product cannot be placed because the current location has become full, the system shall state this against that product and shall route the operator into the location exception handling defined in Section 40.
FR-027.23: Quantity to Bin and Actual Binned shall be recorded as separate values against each binning transaction.
Note: the wrong location and wrong part alerts at FR-027.6 and FR-027.8 are unchanged. The exception handling introduced in Section 40 concerns a correct location that cannot accept the stock, which is a different condition from the operator being at the wrong place.
{Heading1} 31. FR-028: Consignment Workflow Tracking and System Action Traceability
{Heading2} 31.1 Overview
Two capabilities were raised in this session that sit above the individual screens. Both were agreed as desirable rather than mandatory for the current phase, and both are recorded here so that they are not lost when the wireframes are built.
The first is a view of where a consignment has reached. The inward process is a workflow, and the path a consignment takes depends on how its information arrived. A search on a consignment number or a ship list reference should return a graphical stepper showing which stage has been reached, in the manner of a parcel tracking view. The operator entering the data is not the audience for this; the person responsible for warehouse operations is, and for them the time spent at each stage is as informative as the stage itself.
The second is traceability of what the system did on the operator's behalf. Across these flows the system generates box reference stickers, prints product barcode stickers, creates unique scan codes and accepts manual entries where scanning was impossible. Each of those is an intervention. Retaining a record of them means that if something is later found to be missing, it is possible to establish where an action was taken and where a gap was filled by hand. For a solution delivered as software as a service this is what allows the system's own contribution to be audited.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Workflow Tracking - A visual indication of how far a consignment has travelled through the process - arrived, unloaded, checked, put away - shown as a progress bar or a set of steps rather than as a status code that means something only to trained staff. The genuinely useful part is the time spent at each step, because that is what turns a status display into a management tool. Knowing a consignment is at the checking stage is mildly interesting. Knowing it has been at the checking stage for eleven hours tells you something is wrong. Over a few weeks the same data shows which step is consistently the slow one, which is usually not the step people assume.
Example: The screen shows a consignment: arrived 08:10, unloaded 09:40 taking 90 minutes, checking started 09:45 and still running at 16:00. Six hours at checking against a normal two. The supervisor investigates and finds one operator was pulled onto another job. Across a month the same view shows checking averages four hours while everyone had assumed unloading was the bottleneck.
Action Traceability - Systems increasingly do things on their own - assigning a location, closing a session, defaulting a value. When one of those automatic decisions turns out to be wrong, somebody has to be able to see what the system did and why, rather than being told the computer did it. This is different from the log of what people did. It is a record of the system's own actions, and it matters most in exactly the situations where trust is lowest - when stock is missing and the staff insist they followed the process. Without it, every automatic behaviour is unarguable, and the usual response is that people stop trusting the automation and start working around it.
Example: Stock appears in a location nobody remembers choosing. The trace shows the system auto-assigned it when the intended shelf was full, following a rule set up eight months earlier that nobody currently working there knew about. The rule is reviewed and changed. Without the trace, the conclusion would have been that an operator put it in the wrong place.
Notifications - An email sent automatically when a consignment reaches a particular point - arrived, checked, variance reported. It matters because the people who need to know are usually not the people watching the screen. The parts manager wants to know a shortage has been found without ringing the warehouse. Accounts want to know goods have been received before they pay. The alternative is somebody remembering to tell them, which works until the day it matters most. The thing to check is whether it can be configured - which stages, and to whom - because a notification on every event to everybody is worse than none at all, and gets filtered into a folder nobody reads within a week.
Example: A variance is finalised showing 6 pieces short. An email goes to the parts manager and to accounts the moment it is confirmed. Accounts hold the payment pending the credit note rather than paying in full and chasing it afterwards. Nobody had to remember to tell them, and the shortage was known about within minutes rather than at month end.
{Heading2} 31.2 Functional Requirements
FR-028.1: The system should provide a workflow view showing the stage a consignment has reached, retrievable by searching on a consignment number or a ship list reference.
FR-028.2: The workflow view should be presented graphically as a stepper along a numbered sequence of stages, so that the current position is visible at a glance.
FR-028.3: The workflow view should record the time spent at each stage, for the benefit of the operations function rather than the operator entering the data.
FR-028.4: The system should retain a trace of the actions it has taken on the operator's behalf, including box reference stickers generated, product barcode stickers printed, unique scan codes created and manual entries accepted where scanning was not possible.
FR-028.5: The action trace should be reviewable so that where something is later found to be missing, the point at which an action was taken or a gap was filled manually can be established.
FR-028.6: Notification by email at defined stages of the workflow is acknowledged as a requirement. The stages, triggers and recipients are to be defined in a subsequent session.
FR-028.7: The capabilities in this section are classified as desirable rather than mandatory for the current phase and are to be scoped for a later release.
{Heading1} 32. FR-029: Location Audit - Approach
{Heading2} 32.1 Overview
Location audit was introduced at the close of the session as the next area of work. Two approaches were stated, and the detailed requirements are to be captured in a subsequent session.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Checking that what the system says is on a shelf is actually on that shelf. Two ways of doing it are needed. A planned audit works through a defined set of locations on a schedule, which is what gets used for a systematic count. A general audit lets someone walk up to any shelf at any time and check it, which is what actually happens when a discrepancy is suspected. Both matter. A system that only supports scheduled counts means an operator who notices something wrong has no way to record it and will simply mention it to someone. One that only supports ad-hoc checking has no way to guarantee every location gets looked at.
Example: A quarterly planned audit covers zone B over three days, shelf by shelf, with the system tracking which have been done. Separately, on a Tuesday, a picker finds four filters where the system says six, and starts a spot check on that location there and then. Both the discrepancy and the systematic count end up in the same records.
{Heading2} 32.2 Functional Requirements
FR-029.1: The system shall support a planned location audit, in which the user goes to a preassigned location, or to the location of preassigned parts, and checks the parts lying in that location.
FR-029.2: The system shall support a generic location audit carried out without planning, in which the user goes to any location and checks all or some of the parts held there.
FR-029.3: Detailed requirements for the location audit, including the planning mechanism, the recording of results and the treatment of discrepancies, are to be captured in a subsequent session.
 [IMG]
Figure 32.2a - Location Audit: the two approaches stated at the close of Session 06 - a planned audit against a preassigned location or preassigned parts, and a generic audit carried out without planning.
{Heading1} 33. FR-030: Nature of Inward Transactions
{Heading2} 33.1 Overview
Everything specified so far has assumed that goods arriving at the warehouse have been purchased from a supplier. That is only one of six reasons a consignment may arrive, and the reason matters: it determines who owns the goods, whether a payment is due against them, and what the consignment is reconciled against.
Purchase covers vendors of several natures - original equipment manufacturers, co-dealers, local suppliers and any other purchase. Branch transfer in covers movement between one warehouse and another within the same business. Sales return covers goods coming back from a customer, whether because the product was not up to standard or for any other reason. Workshop or in-house return covers parts issued for usage and not consumed - a workshop that drew five piston springs and used three returns the remaining two to the warehouse to be put back into stock.
Job work return covers goods coming back from an outward job work transaction. Where a repair requires welding, polishing or milling that the service centre is not equipped to perform, a job work identifier is raised, the item is sent out to a third party for the process, and it returns to the warehouse afterwards. The inward transaction exists so that the return leg can be monitored against the outward one.
Third party purchase is the one case in which the goods are not owned by the business at all. A contractor operating within the customer's premises - a painting contractor, for example, whose service the service centre does not perform itself - may store their own materials in space taken within the warehouse, on whatever commercial terms have been agreed. The ownership of that inventory remains entirely with the contractor, who draws on it themselves. It is recorded through the same inward process specifically so that contractor stock is kept separate from the business's own and the two do not mix.
Note: the ownership distinction is the one with the widest consequence. Third-party stock occupies warehouse locations and passes through the same gate entry, unloading and binning flows, but must never be counted as the business's inventory, valued in its stock reports, or included in a claim against a supplier.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Transaction Natures - Goods arrive for more reasons than purchases from suppliers, and each reason has to be treated differently by stock and by accounts. Stock coming from another branch is not a purchase and does not create a liability. A part returned by a customer goes back into stock but may need inspection first. A part coming back unused from the workshop is not new stock at all. Something returned from an outside contractor may have been worked on. If the system only understands purchases, everything else gets recorded as a purchase and the accounts are wrong from the start - or it gets recorded outside the system entirely, which is worse. The type is chosen at the gate, at the start, because it affects everything that follows.
Example: Four vehicles arrive in one morning: a supplier delivery, a transfer from the branch across town, a customer returning an unused alternator, and parts coming back from an outside machining shop. Each is recorded under its own type at the gate. The supplier delivery creates a payable, the branch transfer does not, the customer return is routed for inspection, and the machining return is matched against what was sent out.
Ownership Segregation - Not everything in a warehouse is owned by the business that runs it. Contractor-owned goods and customer property sit on the same racks as your own stock and must not be counted as yours. If the system does not distinguish them, they inflate the stock valuation, they appear in reports as available to sell, and eventually somebody sells one. Recording ownership at the point of receipt is what keeps the two apart, and it has to be at receipt because that is the only moment anyone knows. Once the goods are on a shelf they look identical to everything else, and nobody will be able to tell six weeks later which alternator belonged to whom.
Example: A contractor sends fifteen alternators to be held for a fleet job. They are received and marked as contractor-owned. They occupy rack D-02 alongside your own stock but do not appear in the stock valuation and cannot be picked for a normal sale. When the fleet job runs, they are issued against the contractor - and the month-end valuation is not fifteen alternators too high.
Downstream Integration - Recording the type of receipt is only useful if the rest of the business acts on it. A purchase has to create a payable, a branch transfer has to reduce stock at the sending branch, a customer return has to affect the original sale. If the warehouse records the type but accounts never see it, somebody has to be told separately - by email, or on a list at month end - and that is where things get missed. The question worth asking is not whether the field exists but whether it flows. A system where the storekeeper picks the right type and an accounts clerk then re-keys the same decision from a printout has the feature in name only, and has added a step rather than removing one.
Example: A branch transfer is received and marked as such. Accounts see it appear as a transfer with no payable raised, and the sending branch stock reduces automatically. Nobody sends an email and nobody re-keys anything. Where the flow is missing, the same receipt would sit in the warehouse system correctly typed while accounts, seeing only a goods receipt, raise a payable to a supplier who never sent anything.
{Heading2} 33.2 Functional Requirements
FR-030.1: The system shall support six natures of inward transaction, selected at gate entry: purchase, branch transfer in, sales return, workshop or in-house return, job work return, and third party purchase.
FR-030.2: Purchase shall accommodate vendors of multiple natures, including original equipment manufacturers, co-dealers, local suppliers and other purchase sources.
FR-030.3: Branch transfer in shall cover the receipt of goods transferred internally from another warehouse within the same business.
FR-030.4: Sales return shall cover goods returned by a customer, and the system shall capture the reason for the return.
FR-030.5: Workshop or in-house return shall cover parts previously issued for usage and returned unconsumed, and the system shall capture the reason for the return and return the parts to stock.
FR-030.6: Job work return shall cover goods returning from an outward job work transaction, and shall be traceable against the job work identifier raised when the item was sent out.
FR-030.7: Third party purchase shall cover goods belonging to a contractor operating within the premises of the customer for the purpose of providing a service.
FR-030.8: For third party purchase the system shall record that ownership of the goods rests with the contractor and not with the business.
FR-030.9: Goods owned by a contractor shall be held distinctly from the business's own inventory and shall not be commingled with it, either physically in reporting terms or in stock valuation.
FR-030.10: The system shall record the ownership of incoming products as either owned by the business or owned by a contractor operating within the premises of the client.
FR-030.11: The nature of the transaction shall be available to downstream processes, so that reconciliation, payment and stock treatment can differ according to why the consignment arrived.
{Heading1} 34. FR-031: Consignment Master Data, Categories and the Others Fallback
{Heading2} 34.1 Overview
The master data that classifies an incoming consignment was set out in full during this session. Supplier categories are expected to include original equipment manufacturer, branch, co-dealer, co-distributor and local. Cartons are classified as multi-carton or single-carton consignments and as multi-SKU or single-SKU. Consignment carriers are transport, courier, own vehicle, individual external person and individual internal person. Products are classified as spare parts, accessories, lubricants, tyres, batteries, stationery, grocery and others. Consignments may be received against a goods receipt, an invoice, a challan, no document at all, or something else.
The product list reflects an intention wider than the current scope. The platform starts with automotive as its primary vertical, and once it has been proven there the intention is to extend to other verticals such as retail and e-commerce, which is why the classification already reaches beyond spare parts to stationery and groceries.
Every one of these lists ends in Others, and the reason is operational rather than tidiness. A category that has not been anticipated will eventually arrive, and if the classification is mandatory and offers no way through, the operator is stopped at the gate. Others provides the way through - but selecting it requires a description to be entered, because an Others bucket full of unlabelled entries cannot later be examined to find the categories that should have existed. With a description, recurring entries can be recognised and promoted into categories of their own.
Categories are defined by the administrator at user level, since in a multi-SKU environment the supplier and product categories that matter differ from one client to another. Retiring a category is handled by making it inactive rather than deleting it. An old vehicle model whose parts no longer move is of no further use going forward, but deleting the category would discard the history attached to it; making it inactive preserves the reference.
Note: the inactive-category mechanism has a data consequence that needs handling at the point of deactivation rather than afterwards. The user is asked where the existing records should go, and may either nominate a destination category or choose to leave them where they are, in which case the data stays against the closed category and remains reportable.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Master Data (FR-031.1 to .6) - Behind every dropdown on these screens is a list that has to be maintained - suppliers, transport companies, document types, categories of product. The lists themselves are unremarkable; what matters is who controls them. A warehouse that can add a new supplier or a new courier firm itself does so in five minutes when the need arises. A warehouse that has to raise a request with the software vendor waits days, and in the meantime the staff record the new supplier as Other, or pick the nearest wrong entry from the list, and the data quietly degrades. This is one of the most reliable indicators of whether a system will still be accurate after two years of use.
Example: The branch starts buying from a new supplier on a Monday. The administrator adds it to the supplier list that morning and the first delivery on Wednesday is recorded correctly. In a system where the vendor controls the list, the same delivery is recorded against Other or against a similar supplier name - and six months later nobody can produce an accurate purchase history for either of them.
Others Fallback - However carefully the categories are set up, something will arrive that does not fit any of them. If there is no way to record it, the operator picks the closest wrong answer and the data is corrupted silently. An Other option prevents that, but only if it demands a description. Other on its own is worthless - a year later there are four hundred receipts categorised as Other and no way to tell what any of them were. Other, plus a typed description, is genuinely useful: the receipt is recorded honestly, and reading through the descriptions periodically tells you which new categories actually need creating. It turns a gap in the data into a list of improvements.
Example: A supplier sends a specialist tool that fits none of the product categories. The operator picks Other and types diagnostic equipment. Over three months eleven receipts are recorded as Other, and seven of them are diagnostic equipment. The administrator creates that as a proper category, and the eleven honest Other entries have paid for themselves.
Master Data (FR-031.11; FR-031.12) - Two small conveniences that both reduce typing at the moment a new supplier is created. Entering the PIN code fills in the town and the state automatically, rather than leaving somebody to type them and occasionally get them wrong - which matters because addresses drive delivery and tax treatment. And category codes are generated by the system rather than invented by whoever is creating the record. Hand-typed codes are where duplicate and inconsistent supplier and category records come from: two people create the same category a week apart with slightly different codes, and from then on reports split across both. Neither feature is impressive on its own; together they are a good sign that the underlying records have been thought about.
Example: A clerk creates a new supplier and types PIN 411019. The town and state fill in on their own, correctly spelt. She saves and the system allocates the category code. A colleague creating a similar supplier the following week gets a consistent code rather than inventing a second one - and the supplier report at year end has one line per supplier rather than two.
Master Data (FR-031.13) - At the very start, the entire supplier list, part list and category list has to get into the system, and there are usually thousands of entries. Loading them from a spreadsheet takes an afternoon. Typing them takes weeks, is done under time pressure by whoever is available, and produces exactly the kind of errors that are hardest to find later - transposed part numbers, misspelt supplier names, duplicated entries. This is worth asking about even though it is a one-time activity, because the quality of that initial load determines how much the system can be trusted for its whole life, and because a system without bulk loading suggests the same thinking applies elsewhere.
Example: A dealer going live has 4,000 part numbers and 300 suppliers. With bulk upload, the existing lists are cleaned in a spreadsheet, loaded in one afternoon, and checked. Without it, three staff type for two weeks alongside their normal work - and eighteen months later people are still finding parts entered twice under slightly different numbers.
Lifecycle Control - Categories and master records fall out of use, but deleting them destroys history. If a supplier category is deleted, every past receipt recorded against it either breaks or silently loses its classification, and reports covering last year stop matching what they said at the time. Switching it off instead means it disappears from the dropdowns for new work while everything historic stays intact and readable. The other half is what happens to records still sitting under it: a good system asks where they should move to rather than leaving them orphaned. That question is easy to answer at the moment of the change and almost impossible to answer six months later.
Example: A dealer stops using a supplier category with 40 active parts under it. The administrator deactivates it rather than deleting, and the system asks where the 40 should go; she reassigns them to the correct category. New receipts no longer see the old one in the list. Last year's reports still show it, still add up, and still match the printed copies in the file.
{Heading2} 34.2 Functional Requirements
FR-031.1: The system shall maintain a supplier category master, with values including original equipment manufacturer, branch, co-dealer, co-distributor and local.
FR-031.2: The system shall classify cartons as multi-carton or single-carton consignments, and as multi-SKU or single-SKU.
FR-031.3: The system shall maintain a consignment carrier master with the values transport, courier, own vehicle, individual external person and individual internal person.
FR-031.4: The system shall maintain a nature of products master with values including spare parts, accessories, lubricants, tyres, batteries, stationery, grocery and others.
FR-031.5: The system shall maintain a document type master covering goods receipt, invoice, challan, receipt without any document, and others.
FR-031.6: The workflow for consignment inward shall comprise consignment receipt recording, truck unload, consignment check for count and quality of packs inside the primary packing, and binning in the warehouse.
FR-031.7: Every classification list shall carry a default Others category, which shall remain at the end of the list.
FR-031.8: Where a product is received which is not covered by any defined category or by any name held in the master, it shall be classified against Others.
FR-031.9: Where Others is selected the user shall be required to enter a description of what the item is. The description shall be mandatory, so that recurring entries can subsequently be identified and promoted into categories of their own.
FR-031.10: Supplier categories and suppliers shall be defined at user level by the administrator, since the categories that apply differ from one user to another in a multi-SKU environment.
FR-031.11: Category codes shall be generated automatically whenever a new category is created.
FR-031.12: While creating a supplier, the state and city shall be populated from the PIN code using Post Office APIs.
FR-031.13: The system shall provide an option to upload the initial list of suppliers, products and supplier names from an Excel or CSV file, for implementation purposes.
FR-031.14: A category shall be capable of being made inactive rather than deleted, so that the historical data attached to it is preserved and remains available for reference.
FR-031.15: Inactive categories shall appear at the bottom of the list where the option to display all categories is selected.
FR-031.16: Where a category is made inactive, the user shall be required to indicate the category into which the existing data in the database is to be moved, or to choose Don't Move.
FR-031.17: Where Don't Move is chosen, the data shall remain in the database against the closed category and shall remain available for reporting purposes.
FR-031.18: Category and supplier master maintenance shall be provided on the web interface.
FR-031.19: The product classification shall accommodate verticals beyond automotive, since the platform is intended to extend to retail, e-commerce and other verticals after the automotive vertical is established.
 [IMG]
Figure 34.2a - BRD extract: the consignment carrier and nature of products classifications and the consignment inward workflow, each list terminating in an Others value.
 [IMG]
Figure 34.2b - BRD extract: supplier category and supplier handling defined at user level by the administrator, auto-generated category codes, PIN code lookup through Post Office APIs, bulk upload for implementation, and the treatment of inactive categories including the move or don't-move decision.
{Heading1} 35. FR-032: Mobile and Web Channel Coverage
{Heading2} 35.1 Overview
The BRD states twice that the gate entry module must be both mobile-based and web-based. The FRD as written describes a handheld application throughout, and no web or desktop surface is described anywhere in it. This is an architecture-level difference rather than a field-level one, and it needs resolving before screens are built on a mobile-only assumption.
The practical case is clearest at gate entry. Nothing is scanned at that point - a gate entry number is being raised for a consignment that has just arrived - and the person raising it may well be working at a desk on a laptop rather than on a handheld terminal on the floor. Elsewhere the opposite holds: scanning, condition capture and binning are done by an operator moving around the warehouse and are inherently handheld activities.
What is required is a screen-by-screen determination of which surface each screen is delivered on. Making that determination explicitly, rather than defaulting to one surface, lets each module be built for the way it is actually used.
Note: some screens will need both. The consignment check summary and the variance report are read by warehouse operations as much as by the operator who produced them, and a report that only exists on a handheld is difficult to review.
{Heading2} Layman Description
The work of a warehouse happens in two quite different places. Anything involving physically handling goods - scanning boxes off a truck, confirming a shelf, counting pieces - has to be on a handheld device, because the person doing it has his hands on the stock and cannot walk to a desk. Anything involving reviewing, deciding or configuring - looking at a queue of exceptions, assigning a location, running a report - is far better on a full screen where the whole picture is visible. A system that only exists on desktop forces operators to write things down and key them in later, which is where accuracy is lost. One that only exists on a handheld makes administration painful and reports unreadable.
Example: The unloading operator works entirely on a handheld: scanning, condition, photographs. The administrator works entirely on a desktop: the exception queue, location assignment, reports. The supervisor uses both - the handheld when walking the floor, the desktop when reviewing the day. Nobody is holding a phone to do a job that needs a keyboard, or standing at a desk for a job that needs to be done at the truck.
{Heading2} 35.2 Functional Requirements
FR-032.1: The system shall explicitly support both a mobile application and a web-based application.
FR-032.2: The gate entry module shall be available on both the mobile and the web surfaces, in line with the BRD requirement.
FR-032.3: Each screen in the inward module shall be assigned to a delivery surface - mobile, web, or both - and the assignment shall be defined before build.
FR-032.4: The scenarios in which a mobile surface is specifically required and those in which a web surface is specifically required shall be identified, so that each module can be built appropriately.
FR-032.5: Screens which involve no scanning and are typically operated from a desk, such as gate entry, shall be available on the web surface.
FR-032.6: Screens which involve scanning, condition capture or physical handling shall be available on the handheld surface.
{Heading1} 36. FR-033: Ship List Capture, Upload and Extraction
{Heading2} 36.1 Overview
The ship list is the baseline against which unloading is reconciled - it states how many boxes and which items the truck should be carrying. In practice it arrives as a hard copy, which raises the question of how it reaches the system.
Three routes were discussed. The supplier may provide a soft copy, in which case the file is uploaded directly. Where only a hard copy exists, the operator may photograph each page through the device camera and the pages are assembled into a PDF. Failing both, the details may be keyed manually.
Manual keying is the least preferred of the three. A ship list for a substantial consignment runs to a long list of line items, and the likelihood of error in transcribing it by hand is high. The preference is for a PDF by either route, from which the system reads the document and extracts the line items into the master data tables.
Note: the uploaded ship list should remain viewable and replaceable after upload. A document photographed on the floor may turn out to be illegible, and the operator needs to be able to see what was captured and replace it without restarting the consignment.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Ship List Capture - The supplier packing list has to get into the system somehow, and there are two realities. Sometimes it arrives as a PDF by email before the truck does, in which case it can simply be uploaded. Often it arrives as a sheet of paper in the driver's hand and nothing else exists. Photographing it page by page with the device camera covers the second case, which is far more common than software vendors tend to assume. If only PDF upload is supported, then every paper packing list either gets typed in by somebody or, more likely, gets ignored entirely - and with it goes the independent expected quantity that makes a shortage claim stand up.
Example: One supplier emails a PDF packing list the night before; it is uploaded in seconds. Another sends a three-page printed list with the driver. The storekeeper photographs all three pages at the bay with the handheld. Both consignments now have an expected quantity recorded from the supplier's own document, and both can be checked properly.
Ship List Extraction - Getting the packing list into the system is only half the job; the numbers on it have to become usable data. Extraction reads the document and pulls out the individual lines - part numbers and quantities - so the system can compare them against what is actually found. The alternative is somebody typing thirty or forty lines while a driver waits, which mostly does not happen, and the list ends up stored as an image nobody ever compares anything to. The question worth asking is what happens when extraction gets it wrong, because it sometimes will: whether the operator can correct a misread line, and whether he is shown what was extracted before it becomes the expected quantity.
Example: A three-page list is photographed and the system extracts 34 lines totalling 214 pieces. Two lines are misread because the print was faint; the operator sees the extracted list on screen, corrects them against the paper, and confirms. Those 34 lines become the expected quantities for the check. Where extraction is not offered, the same list would sit on file as three photographs and the consignment would be checked against nothing.
{Heading2} 36.2 Functional Requirements
FR-033.1: The system shall provide an option to upload the ship list for a consignment.
FR-033.2: The ship list shall be uploadable in PDF format, since the supplier is expected to be able to provide a soft copy in most cases.
FR-033.3: Where only a hard copy is available, the system shall allow each page to be captured through the device camera and shall assemble the captured pages into a PDF.
FR-033.4: The system shall read the uploaded or captured PDF and extract the line item details into the master data tables.
FR-033.5: Manual entry of the ship list details shall remain available, but shall be treated as the least preferred route because of the high likelihood of transcription error on a long list.
FR-033.6: An uploaded ship list shall remain viewable after upload, and shall be replaceable where the captured document is illegible or incorrect.
FR-033.7: The extracted ship list shall provide the expected box and item counts against which the truck unload is reconciled.
{Heading2} 36.3 Screen Field Reference - Ship List Capture and Reconciliation
Note: these controls sit on the unload screen rather than on a screen of their own. The ship list is uploaded at the point it is needed - when the truck is about to be unloaded against it - and the reconciliation is visible in the same place.
[TABLE]
| Field | Control Type | Description |
| Shipment Details (Shiplist) | Upload panel | Shows the uploaded ship list file with its name and the date and time it was uploaded. |
| View / Replace | Actions | Opens the uploaded document for inspection or replaces it where the captured copy is illegible. |
| Shipment Information Unavailable | Warning banner | Displayed where no shipment document is available, stating that boxes cannot be reconciled against a list. |
| Expected Boxes | Table with view-all | The box references drawn from the extracted ship list, each shown against the document it belongs to and its current status. |
| Scan or Enter Primary Box | Scan field | Captures each box as it comes off the truck and matches it against the expected list. |
| Mismatch with Shiplist | Alert panel | Raised where a scanned box is not listed in the uploaded ship list, offering Set Aside or Add Document as the two ways forward. |
| WIP Status | Counter row | Total, unloaded, acceptable and not acceptable counts for the session in progress. |
| Unload Progress | Progress bar | Boxes unloaded against boxes expected, expressed as a percentage of the ship list. |
[/TABLE]
{Heading1} 37. FR-034: Warehouse Location Planning and Wave Picking (Forward-Looking)
{Heading2} 37.1 Overview
A forward-looking capability was described towards the end of the session and is recorded here so that it is not lost, on the understanding that it sits beyond the current inward scope.
The intention is that the system should inform where products are placed rather than simply recording where they were put. If fast-moving products are known, they can be positioned in the early part of a picking route so that they are quick to reach, and the layout of the warehouse becomes something that is planned rather than inherited.
The same information supports picking. Rather than issuing an order to a single picker to walk the whole warehouse, the system can group work into waves and send several pickers out at once, each covering part of the order, so that the order is completed within its time window.
Note: this depends on movement data the system will only hold once it has been running for some time. It is a later-phase capability, but the location and binning data being captured now is what will make it possible.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Location Planning - Where a part is stored ought to depend on how often it is picked. Fast-moving items near the front and at waist height save a few seconds on every single pick, and across a year that is a substantial amount of walking. Slow-moving items can go to the back or up high. Doing this properly means the system watches how often each part actually moves and suggests relocating those in the wrong place. Most warehouses know this in principle and never act on it, because working out which parts to move requires data nobody has time to assemble. A system that produces the suggestion turns a good idea into something that actually happens.
Example: The system reports that a wiper blade picked forty times a month sits at the back of aisle D, while a part picked twice a year occupies a prime position by the door. It suggests swapping them. The change takes ten minutes and saves the picker a two-hundred-metre round trip forty times a month.
Wave Picking - Rather than picking one order at a time, several are grouped into a batch and collected in a single walk through the warehouse, with the system deciding the sequence so that the route runs in one direction rather than doubling back. On a large site this is the single biggest saving available in picking, because most of the time is spent walking rather than picking. It only makes sense above a certain size: in a small store where everything is within twenty metres, the batching overhead costs more than the walking it saves. Worth establishing not only whether a system offers it but at what size of operation it starts to be worth switching on.
Example: Six orders need eighteen parts between them, scattered across four aisles. Picked individually that is six separate walks. Batched, the system produces one route through the four aisles collecting all eighteen, then sorts them into the six orders at the packing bench. One walk instead of six.
{Heading2} 37.2 Functional Requirements
FR-034.1: The system should support location planning informed by product movement, identifying fast-moving products and recommending where they are placed.
FR-034.2: Fast-moving products should be positioned early in the picking route so that they are quick to reach.
FR-034.3: The system should support wave-based picking, in which picking work is grouped and issued to several pickers at once so that an order is completed within its time window.
FR-034.4: The system should determine the direction and sequence in which a picking route is walked.
FR-034.5: The capabilities in this section are forward-looking and sit outside the current inward scope. They are recorded for scoping in a later phase.
{Heading1} 38. FR-035: Role Hierarchy, Access Control and Audit Logging
{Heading2} 38.1 Overview
The system is delivered to more than one kind of organisation and more than one kind of user within each, and the BRD sets out a role hierarchy accordingly. At the top sits the Sparecare solution administrator, who administers the platform itself. Beneath that sits the dealer administrator, who configures the system for their own organisation - which fields appear, which categories exist, which workflows apply, which permissions users hold. Beneath that sits the dealer user, who carries out the warehouse work.
The principle attaching to this hierarchy is that every action taken by any of these roles creates an auditable log. That was stated as a principle in the BRD but has not until now been set out in this document, even though individual requirements throughout it reference the dealer administrator configuring one thing or another.
The requirement is deliberately broad. Actions are to be logged for every person, not only for administrators and not only for configuration changes - the operational actions matter as much, since it is the operational record that is examined when a discrepancy is investigated.
Note: this section exists because the permissions were being specified one at a time as each screen was discussed - who may bin without a location, who may key a code manually, who may hide an expected quantity. Those are individual expressions of a single access control model, and the model itself needs to be defined before the individual permissions can be assigned coherently.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Access Control - Different people should be able to do different things. A gate guard needs to create arrivals and nothing else. An unloading operator needs to scan and record condition but has no business changing the supplier or category lists. A supervisor can override locations and permit manual entry. An administrator can define locations and change the underlying lists. The important question is not whether roles exist - almost every system claims them - but how finely they can be drawn and who controls them. If there are only two levels, user and administrator, then everybody who needs to do one supervisory task gets full administrative rights, and the control is decorative.
Example: A new operator joins and is given the unloading role: he can scan boxes and record condition, but the option to type in a location is not on his screen at all. His supervisor has that option, plus the ability to approve exceptions. Neither can add a supplier - that sits with the administrator. Three different screens for the same system, and nobody has rights they do not need.
Audit Logging - A record of who did what and when, kept automatically as people work. Which operator scanned a particular box, who changed a condition from OK to damaged, who overrode a location, who deactivated a category. Nobody looks at it most of the time, and that is the point - it is there for the day something has gone wrong and the question is not what happened but how. Without it, a stock discrepancy is a mystery that produces an argument. With it, it is a specific event with a time and a name attached, and the conversation is about fixing a process rather than about who is to blame. Worth asking how far back it goes and whether an administrator can alter it.
Example: A part is recorded in B-12-04 but is not there. The log shows it was scanned into B-12-04 at 11:42 by one operator, and that the record was edited at 16:10 by someone else who changed the quantity. The conversation becomes about why quantities were being edited at four in the afternoon, rather than five people insisting they did not touch it.
{Heading2} 38.2 Functional Requirements
FR-035.1: The system shall implement a role hierarchy comprising, at minimum, the Sparecare solution administrator, the dealer administrator and the dealer user.
FR-035.2: The Sparecare solution administrator shall administer the platform across dealer organisations.
FR-035.3: The dealer administrator shall configure the system for their own organisation, including field visibility, category masters, workflow defaults and the permissions held by users at that dealer.
FR-035.4: The dealer user shall carry out warehouse operations within the permissions granted by the dealer administrator.
FR-035.5: All actions taken by any role shall create auditable logs. Logging shall not be confined to administrative or configuration actions.
FR-035.6: Audit logs shall record the action taken, the person who took it, and the time at which it was taken.
FR-035.7: The permission-gated behaviours specified elsewhere in this document shall be expressed as permissions within this access control model, including binning where no location exists (FR-026.15 and FR-026.16), manual entry of unscannable codes at binning (FR-027.14), manual entry of a bin location during unloading (FR-025.14), and hiding the expected quantity to bin (FR-026.17).
FR-035.8: The full role set, and the permissions attaching to each role, are to be defined before build.
{Heading1} 39. FR-036: Terminology Standardisation and Field Naming
{Heading2} 39.1 Overview
Two terminology corrections were made during the review, both of which apply across the whole document and the wireframes rather than to a single screen.
The first is the term Inward Code. It appears in several screen labels and drill-down descriptions as an alternative name for the gate entry number. There is no such thing as an inward code: there is only the gate entry number. The term is to be removed entirely rather than retained as a synonym, because carrying two names for one identifier means every reader has to work out whether they are the same thing.
The second is the Courier Type value recorded as Self-foot, which is not a mode of transport. It arose as a transcription artefact and has been withdrawn, with the value list aligned to the five values defined in the BRD.
Note: both corrections have been applied throughout this document, including in figure captions and field reference tables. They also need applying to the wireframes, where Inward Code appears as a screen label on several boards - the FRD and the wireframes will otherwise disagree with each other.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Over the life of a project the same thing gets called by different names - one screen says one word, the specification says another, and the wireframes a third. Nobody notices while the project is small, and by the time it is large every conversation starts with working out what the other person means. This settles on one word for each thing and applies it everywhere, so the screen, the specification and the people building it all use the same vocabulary. It is not a feature anyone uses; it is housekeeping that saves an argument a week once the system is live.
Example: The identifier created when a vehicle arrives was being called an inward code on some screens and a gate entry number on others, and new joiners assumed they were two different things. One term was chosen, and every screen, requirement and wireframe now uses it. A developer reading the specification and an operator reading the screen see the same word.
{Heading2} 39.2 Functional Requirements
FR-036.1: The term Inward Code shall not be used. The identifier shall be referred to as the Gate Entry Number (GEN) throughout the system, the documentation and the wireframes.
FR-036.2: No synonym or alternative name shall be introduced for the gate entry number.
FR-036.3: Existing screen labels reading GEN / Inward Code shall be changed to Gate Entry Number (GEN).
FR-036.4: The Courier Type value Self-foot is withdrawn. The value list shall carry the five values defined in the BRD, per FR-001.20.
FR-036.5: Terminology corrections agreed in review shall be applied to the wireframes as well as to this document, so that the two do not diverge.
{Heading1} 40. FR-037: Binning Location Exception Handling - Screens 16A to 16F
{Heading2} 40.1 Overview
Binning assumes that the location recorded against a part can accept the stock being placed. In practice it often cannot. The location may have filled since it was last looked at, because stock moves in and out continuously and the operator has no view of what has happened there in the interim. The part may have no location defined in the master at all. Or the location may be full and the alternates recorded against the part may themselves be full.
The same three conditions arise in all three binning routes, and the same resolution applies in each: offer the alternate locations already mapped against the part, permit a location outside that list only where the user is authorised to enter one, and where neither is possible raise a request to the administrator so that the location is defined or corrected. The operator is never left holding stock with no route forward.
Whether a location is full is established by the operator at the location. The system does not hold the capacity of a location or how much of it is occupied, so it cannot determine this itself. The operator arrives, sees there is no space, and says so.
Note: these screens carry the labels Screen 13A/13B, 14A/14B and 15A/15B on the source boards. Screens 13A to 13D are already assigned to Case 2D in Section 27, and 14a, 14b and 15 to the binning routes in Section 30, so the source labels cannot be carried into this document without colliding. The exception handling screens are therefore numbered 16A to 16F here, and the boards are to be relabelled to match. See OI-076.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Location Exception Handling (FR-037.1 to .6) - The shelf the system sends you to is sometimes already full - stock moves in and out all day and the system does not know what has happened there since this morning. Rather than leaving the operator standing there, it offers the other shelves already approved for that part, and marks which of them have room. He picks one, scans it to confirm he is really there, and carries on. The two details that matter are whether the alternatives are shown immediately - as against him having to go back to a menu and start again - and whether the availability is shown, since a list of five alternatives with no indication of which are usable just means visiting them until one works.
Example: The operator arrives at B-12-04 with two brake discs and it is packed solid. The screen offers B-12-05, marked available, and B-13-01, marked full. He walks to B-12-05, scans it, and the system confirms binning will continue there. Total delay, about ninety seconds - against a walk back to the office to ask somebody.
Location Exception Handling (FR-037.7 to .9) - When none of the approved shelves will work, the last resort is to put the stock somewhere else entirely and type that location in. This is deliberately restricted, because it is the one action that puts stock in a place the system was never told about. Whether an operator can do it should depend on his role: an experienced storekeeper choosing a sensible overflow shelf is reasonable, a new man picking somewhere at random is not. Whoever does it, the location chosen is recorded, and recorded specifically as a manual choice rather than a normal placement. That flag is what lets a supervisor review it afterwards and either approve the location properly or move the stock before it is forgotten.
Example: Every approved shelf for a bulky bumper is full. A senior storekeeper is permitted to enter another location, types D-01-07, and the stock is recorded there and flagged as manually chosen. The next morning the supervisor reviews the flagged entries, agrees D-01-07 is sensible for bumpers, and adds it to the approved list - so the next one is not an exception at all.
Location Exception Handling (FR-037.10 to .12) - A part with no shelf defined anywhere cannot simply be put down and forgotten. The operator puts it in a temporary place, records where, and raises the question with an administrator. The important half is what happens to the rest of his work: he sets that one item aside and carries on with everything else on the trolley. A system that blocks the whole trolley until the question is answered turns one unknown part into an operator standing idle and eleven other parts still unstored. The temporary location is recorded rather than left informal, so when the administrator answers an hour later the stock can be found and moved rather than hunted for on the floor.
Example: A new part arrives with no location in the system. The operator puts it on the overflow rack, records that as temporary, and sends a request to the administrator. He then finishes the other eleven items on his trolley normally. At 3 pm the administrator assigns C-04-05, the operator is notified, and he moves the one part across. Nothing waited except the item that genuinely had to.
Location Exception Handling (FR-037.13; FR-037.14) - Every one of these exceptions - a full shelf, a typed-in location, an alternative chosen, a request raised - is written to a record that can be looked at afterwards. Individually each is trivial and nobody would review them. In aggregate they are the most useful diagnostic a warehouse manager has, because they show where the system's picture of the warehouse has drifted away from the warehouse itself. A location that is full every week is under-allocated. A part that raises a request every time it arrives has never been set up properly. An operator typing locations far more than his colleagues either has a broken scanner or a habit worth asking about. None of that is visible from the individual events.
Example: Over a month the log shows 62 exceptions. Nineteen are the same location being full, twelve are the same three parts having no location defined, and eight are one operator entering locations manually. Three specific fixes come out of that: reallocate one rack, set up three parts properly, and check one scanner - and the following month the count falls to eleven.
{Heading2} 40.2 Functional Requirements
FR-037.1: The system shall provide location exception handling at the point of binning in all three binning routes: binning by product (Screens 16A and 16B), binning by location (Screens 16C and 16D) and direct binning (Screens 16E and 16F).
FR-037.2: The system shall recognise three exception conditions: the mapped location is full; no alternate mapped location is available; and no bin location is defined for the part.
FR-037.3: A location shall be treated as full when the operator declares it full at the location. The system shall not infer fullness, since the capacity of a location and the space remaining in it are not held (see Section 42).
FR-037.4: Where the mapped location is full, the system shall display the alternate locations recorded against the part, each marked as available or full, and shall allow the operator to select an available one.
FR-037.5: The selected alternate location shall be scanned and confirmed at the bin in the same way as a primary location, as required by FR-027.5, before the placement is recorded.
FR-037.6: On confirmation the system shall state that binning will continue at the alternate location, so that the operator has positive confirmation before placing the stock.
FR-037.7: Where no alternate mapped location is available, the system shall offer entry of a location outside the mapped list and notification of the administrator.
FR-037.8: Entry of a location outside the mapped list shall be permitted only where the user holds the corresponding permission, consistent with the role-based control at FR-026.14 to FR-026.16 and the manual entry permission at FR-027.14.
FR-037.9: Where a location outside the mapped list is used, the system shall record the location chosen so that the master can be corrected against it.
FR-037.10: Where no bin location is defined for the part, the system shall offer entry of a temporary location and the raising of a request to the administrator.
FR-037.11: A request raised to the administrator shall carry a request identifier, the part, the reason for the exception, the user raising it and its status, and the operator shall be shown this summary at the point of raising it.
FR-037.12: The operator shall be able to set the item aside and continue binning the remainder of the cart while a request is outstanding. Binning shall not be blocked at the cart level by a single unresolved item.
FR-037.13: All exceptions, manual entries, alternate locations chosen and administrator requests shall be captured in the audit trail.
FR-037.14: Direct binning shall follow the same exception handling as the cart-based routes, without cart formation.
