{Heading1} 41. FR-038: Binning Location Exception Queue - Administrator - Screen 17
{Heading2} 41.1 Overview
Every request raised under Section 40 arrives in a single administrator queue. The administrator sees what could not be binned and why, defines or corrects the location, and releases the operator to continue. Until that happens the stock is standing somewhere unrecorded, so the queue exists to keep that period short and visible rather than to file the exceptions.
The administrator can do more than answer the immediate request. Where the part has no location in the master, the location decided here can be written back to the master so that the next consignment of the same part does not raise the same exception. Where the answer is provisional, the request can be marked as pending a master update and the operator allowed to bin temporarily in the meantime.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Administrator Exception Queue (FR-038.1 to .6) - Every request an operator raises arrives on one screen for an administrator to deal with. It shows the total waiting and breaks it down by type - no location defined, location full, no alternatives available - because those three need different responses and it helps to see the shape of the backlog before opening anything. Filters by zone, supplier, priority and status let one person work a particular area or clear the urgent ones first, and a search by part number or request reference handles the case where somebody rings up about a specific item. The reason this screen matters is that every request on it represents stock standing somewhere temporary. The queue exists to keep that period short and visible, not to file the problems away.
Example: The administrator opens the screen at 9 am: 18 open requests - 7 no location defined, 6 location full, 5 no alternatives. She filters to zone B, clears the four there in ten minutes, then works the high-priority ones across the other zones. By 10 am the count is down to six, and four operators who were blocked have been released.
Administrator Exception Queue (FR-038.7 to .11) - From the same screen the administrator gives the part a proper home, specifying it down through the warehouse structure - zone, then sub-zone, then rack, then the position on the rack - rather than typing a free-text location that may or may not exist. The second and more valuable part is saving that decision back into the part records permanently. Answering the immediate request unblocks one operator today. Writing it back means the next consignment of that part goes straight to the shelf with no exception at all. Without the write-back the same request is raised every time the part arrives, and the administrator answers the same question every month for years.
Example: A part has raised the same no-location request on three consecutive deliveries. The administrator assigns Zone B, sub-zone 2, rack 12, position 04, and ticks update the part records. The operator is released immediately, and when the part arrives again six weeks later it shows B-12-04 on the screen at cart assembly. The request is never raised again.
Administrator Exception Queue (FR-038.12 to .15) - When the administrator has decided, the operator who raised the request is told automatically - he does not have to keep checking, and he is not left waiting for a phone call. That closes the loop properly: raised, answered, resumed. There is also a middle option for when the answer is provisional. The administrator can permit the stock to be put away temporarily while the permanent location is still being worked out, and mark the request as awaiting a proper decision. Without that middle path, everything has to be either fully resolved or left blocked, and in practice somebody will make an unrecorded decision on the floor to keep the work moving.
Example: At 3 pm the administrator assigns a location and clicks notify. The operator gets a message saying the part now has a home and he can put it away. For a second request she is not yet sure where the part belongs long term, so she permits temporary binning on the overflow rack and marks it pending a proper decision - the stock is off the floor and recorded, and the question stays open on her list rather than being quietly dropped.
{Heading2} 41.2 Functional Requirements
FR-038.1: The system shall provide an administrator screen presenting all location exceptions raised during binning.
FR-038.2: The queue shall show counts of open requests and of requests by exception type: no location defined, location full, and no alternatives available.
FR-038.3: The queue shall be filterable by exception type, zone, supplier, priority and status, and searchable by part number and request identifier.
FR-038.4: Each request in the queue shall show the request identifier, part number, description, quantity, recommended location, exception type, the cart it was raised from, the user who raised it, the time it was raised and its status.
FR-038.5: Request status shall distinguish at minimum open, pending review and resolved.
FR-038.6: Requests shall carry a priority, so that the administrator can work the queue in order of urgency rather than in order of arrival.
FR-038.7: Selecting a request shall present its detail: part, product description, pack size and quantity to bin, binning zone, the currently suggested location, the exception, the binning route and cart it was raised from, and the user and time it was raised.
FR-038.8: The administrator shall be able to define a primary location and alternate locations for the part, specifying zone, sub-zone, rack and location.
FR-038.9: The administrator shall be able to record a reason or remark against the decision.
FR-038.10: The administrator shall be able to permit temporary manual binning for the request, so that the operator can place the stock before the master is settled.
FR-038.11: The administrator shall be able to write the decided location back to the part-location master, so that the exception does not recur for the same part.
FR-038.12: The administrator shall be able to publish the location and notify the user, and the user shall be notified that the part now has a defined location and that binning can proceed.
FR-038.13: The administrator shall be able to mark a request as pending a master update where the location given is provisional.
FR-038.14: The queue shall record the resolution, the resolving user and the date and time against each request.
FR-038.15: The exception workflow shall be presented as three stages: the exception is raised during binning, the administrator defines or updates the location, and the user resumes binning.
{Heading2} 41.3 Screen Field Reference - Screen 17 (Binning Location Exception Queue)
Note: Screen 17 is the administrator view. It is a web screen rather than a handheld one, consistent with the channel assignment in Section 35. Field descriptions in blue italic are analyst-generated.
[TABLE]
| Field / Section | Control Type | Description |
| Exception Type / Zone / Supplier / Priority / Status | Dropdown filters | Narrow the queue to a subset of requests. All default to All. |
| Search Part No. / Request ID | Search field | Direct retrieval of a known request or of every request against a part. |
| Open Requests / No Location Defined / Location Full / No Alternatives | Summary counters | Volume by exception type, giving the administrator the shape of the backlog before opening anything. |
| Location Exception Requests | Table | The queue itself. Columns: Request ID, Part No., Description, Qty, Recommended Location, Exception Type, Current Cart, Raised By, Time, Status. |
| Recent / WIP Requests | Side list | Recently raised or in-progress requests with their priority, for direct access without filtering. |
| Request Details | Read-only panel | Full detail of the selected request, including the binning route it was raised from and the cart number. |
| Primary Location / Alternative Locations | Selection controls | The location the administrator assigns to the part and any alternates to be mapped alongside it. |
| Zone / Sub-Zone / Rack / Location | Selection and entry | The location hierarchy through which the assignment is specified. |
| Capacity / Available Space | Numeric entry | Space available at the location. Present on the board but not yet agreed as in scope - see Section 42 and OI-075. |
| Reason / Remarks | Text entry | The administrator’s note against the decision, retained with the request. |
| Allow temporary manual binning | Checkbox | Permits the operator to place the stock before the master is settled, per FR-038.10. |
| Update part-location master | Checkbox | Writes the decided location back to the master so the exception does not recur, per FR-038.11. |
| Save &amp; Publish Location | Primary action | Commits the assignment and makes it available to the operator. |
| Notify User | Action button | Tells the user who raised the request that binning can proceed. |
| Mark Pending Master Update | Action button | Flags the request as answered provisionally, pending a master change. |
| Exception Workflow | Process indicator | The three stages defined at FR-038.15, shown so the administrator can see where the request stands. |
[/TABLE]
 [IMG]
Figure 41.3a - Screen 17: the administrator exception queue with counts by exception type, the request table, the request detail and admin action panel, and the three-stage exception workflow
{Heading1} 42. FR-039: Location Recommendation and Alternate Location Rules
{Heading2} 42.1 Overview
The binning screens present a recommended location and a set of alternates. The basis on which those are derived has not been defined, and the session established that it must be before the screens can be built.
Two bases were discussed. The first is history: where parts of this kind have been placed before, so that the next consignment goes where the last one went and the alternate follows the same pattern. The second is proximity: recommending the location nearest to where the operator already is, so that a cart carrying several parts is worked in a route that moves forward rather than doubling back. The second matters because the operator is walking a cart around a warehouse, and a recommendation that sends them five rows back costs real time.
Both depend on knowing whether a location can take the stock, and the system does not hold that. Location capacity and the space remaining in a location are not captured today. If they were, a full location would never be recommended and the exception handling in Section 40 would arise far less often. Capturing them was described as an added-value capability a dealer might ask for rather than as part of the current scope.
Current practice fills the gap informally. Warehouse staff place stock where they can see space, and an experienced operator carries a working knowledge of the racking that the system has no access to. It varies between dealers and it changes over time.
Note: no rule is specified here because none was agreed. What is recorded is the requirement that the rules be defined, the two candidate bases put forward, and the position on capacity. OI-074 tracks the derivation rules and OI-075 the capacity question, including the Capacity / Available Space field that appears on the Screen 17 board ahead of any agreement that capacity is in scope.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Location Fullness - Whether a shelf is full is decided by the person standing in front of it, not by the system. The system does not know how much space a location has or how much of it is used, so it cannot work this out - and pretending otherwise would produce confident wrong answers. The operator sees there is no room and says so. What matters is how he says it. It should be a single tap, not a form: these are people working quickly with gloves on and often with limited literacy, and anything requiring typing will be skipped in favour of squeezing the stock in somewhere it does not belong. The moment he declares it full, the alternatives should appear immediately rather than sending him back to a menu.
Example: The operator arrives at C-04-02 with five oil filters and there is no room at all. He taps the full marker once - no typing, no explanation required. The alternatives appear on the same screen straight away, he picks C-04-05, and he is putting stock away within fifteen seconds of arriving at a shelf he could not use.
Recommendation Rules - The system suggests where a part should go, but the basis for that suggestion is a design decision with real consequences, and it is worth asking directly rather than accepting that a recommendation appears. There are two sensible approaches. History-based means sending the part where parts like it have gone before, which keeps the warehouse consistent and predictable. Proximity-based means sending the operator to the nearest suitable shelf, which matters because he is walking a trolley and a suggestion that sends him five rows back costs real time on every trip. Neither is obviously right and many systems do neither, simply showing a location that was typed in once and never reviewed.
Example: An operator with a trolley of twelve parts is in aisle B. A proximity-based system finishes aisle B first, then C, then D - one loop. A history-based system might send him B, D, B, C in the order the parts were loaded, adding several minutes per trolley and a good deal of walking across a shift. Ask which it does, and ask to watch a trolley being worked.
{Heading2} 42.2 Functional Requirements
FR-039.1: The rules by which a recommended location is derived shall be defined before the binning screens are implemented.
FR-039.2: The rules by which alternate locations are derived and ordered shall be defined on the same basis.
FR-039.3: Location capacity and remaining space are not captured by the system in the current scope. The recommendation shall not depend on them unless and until they are.
FR-039.4: A location shall be recorded as full only on the declaration of the operator at the location, as established at FR-037.3.
FR-039.5: Where the operator declares a location full, the system shall present the alternate locations for the part immediately, without requiring the operator to navigate away and return.
FR-039.6: The means by which the operator declares a location full shall be a single control requiring no typing, so that it remains usable by staff working at speed and by staff with limited literacy.
{Heading1} 43. FR-040: Forward-Looking Binning Enhancements
{Heading2} 43.1 Overview
Four capabilities were discussed at length and none was agreed. They are recorded here because the reasoning against them is as useful as the ideas themselves, and because the same suggestions will otherwise be made again.
The first is a site map view: a schematic of the warehouse showing where the operator is, where the recommended location lies and where the alternates lie. Not satellite navigation and not a dynamic route, but a plan view the operator can read. Its value depends entirely on the size of the site. In a warehouse of several floors and long corridors an operator can genuinely lose their way; at a dealer with open ground and a single storeroom it would be an overkill.
The second is voice interaction. On the floor, instruction passes verbally between a supervisor and the operator, and none of it reaches the system. The proposal was to capture that exchange - the operator states where stock has been placed, or asks where to place it, and the system records it. The objections were practical. Warehouse staff speak many languages and accents, and a system that mishears is worse than no system. The counter-example offered was parcel delivery, where the courier completes three or four simple actions and voice plays no part. The conclusion reached was that usability decides it: a capability that is not used has no value, whatever its sophistication.
The third is cart tracking: the operator starts a cart, and its movement becomes visible to a supervisor in the way a ride in progress is visible to a dispatcher. The fourth is cart splitting, where the stock on one cart is placed across two locations because no single location can take it all.
Note: none of these is specified as a requirement. They are recorded as capability candidates with the reasoning attached, following the treatment of location planning and wave picking in Section 37. The session closed with these to be reviewed with a senior stakeholder before the location audit work begins.
{Heading2} Layman Description
Navigation Aids - A plan of the warehouse on the device showing where the operator is, where the shelf he needs is, and where the alternatives are. Not turn-by-turn navigation and not a live moving map - just a readable layout, the way a shopping centre directory shows you where you are standing. Its value depends almost entirely on the size of the site. In a warehouse with several floors and long corridors, a new operator genuinely does get lost and this saves real time. In a dealer's store with one room and open ground, it is an overkill that nobody would open twice. Worth asking whether a competitor offers it and, more usefully, which of their customers actually switch it on.
Example: A new operator on his third day is sent to F-02-14 in a warehouse covering three floors. The plan shows him he is on the wrong floor and where the stairs are. He gets there in two minutes instead of ten minutes and asking two people. In a single-room store the same operator can see every shelf from where he is standing.
Voice Interaction - Speaking to the system rather than typing - telling it where stock has been placed, or asking where something should go, with the answer coming back by voice. The attraction is obvious: the operator has his hands full and a device in his pocket. The difficulty is language. Warehouse staff speak many languages and many accents, and a system that mishears is worse than no system, because a wrong location entered confidently is harder to find than no entry at all. The comparison worth making is with parcel delivery, where the courier completes three or four simple taps and voice plays no part - simplicity beat sophistication. If a competitor offers this, the question is whether it is genuinely used.
Example: An operator with an armful of parts says he has put a bracket in C-04-05 and the system records it. It works well for him. A colleague with a different regional accent is misheard twice in a morning, records stock in the wrong location once, and reverts to typing. The feature exists and half the shift has stopped using it - which is a Partial, not a Yes.
Cart Tracking - A supervisor being able to see, on a screen, which trolleys are out on the floor and what stage each has reached - in the way a dispatcher watches vehicles on a map. On a large site this answers questions that are otherwise answered by walking around: is anyone free, has that urgent putaway been done, why has trolley 3 not moved for forty minutes. It becomes worth having roughly at the point where the supervisor can no longer see the whole floor from where he stands. Below that size it is a screen showing him what he can already see out of the window, and it will not be looked at.
Example: At 11 am the supervisor sees five trolleys active. Four are moving through their locations normally. Trolley 3 has not moved since 10:20 - the operator hit a full shelf and is waiting on an exception. The supervisor resolves it from his desk without anyone having walked over to find him.
Cart Splitting - Where a trolley carries more stock of one part than any single location can take, the operator splits it across two places and the system keeps track of both. Without it he faces three bad options: force the stock in where it does not fit, carry the remainder back to the bay, or put it somewhere else and not record it. The last is the most likely and the most damaging. This is closely related to the exception handling for full locations, and the two are worth asking about together - a system that handles a full shelf but cannot split a quantity has only solved half the problem.
Example: An operator carries thirty brake discs for B-12-04, which will take eighteen. He records 18 there and the system asks where the remaining 12 are going; he takes them to B-12-09 and records those. Stock shows 18 and 12 in two locations, which is what a person walking the aisle would count.
Cart Composition - At present the operator decides what goes on a trolley - usually whatever came off the truck together. The system could instead decide, grouping parts so that one trolley serves one area of the warehouse rather than sending the operator to six aisles. The saving is in walking, and on a large site it is considerable. It also changes who needs to know the warehouse: at the moment an experienced operator loads a sensible trolley because he knows the layout, and a new one loads a poor trolley and walks twice as far. Having the system group them makes the new operator as efficient as the experienced one on his first day.
Example: Twenty parts come off a truck for eight different aisles. Loaded by hand they go onto two trolleys in the order they were unloaded, and each trolley visits six or seven aisles. Grouped by the system, the same twenty parts go onto three trolleys covering two or three adjacent aisles each - more trolleys, but far less walking, and no knowledge of the layout required.
{Heading2} 43.2 Capability Candidates
FR-040.1: A schematic site map showing the operator position, the recommended location and the alternate locations is recorded as a candidate capability. It shall be scoped by site size rather than offered universally.
FR-040.2: Voice capture of instruction and confirmation on the warehouse floor is recorded as a candidate capability, subject to demonstrated usability across the languages and accents in actual use.
FR-040.3: Cart movement tracking, giving a supervisor visibility of carts in progress, is recorded as a candidate capability.
FR-040.4: Splitting the contents of a cart across more than one location where no single location can accept it is recorded as a candidate capability.
FR-040.5: System-directed cart composition, in which the system determines which parts are grouped onto which cart so that a cart serves a single area, is recorded as a candidate capability. Cart composition is currently determined by the operator.
FR-040.6: None of the capabilities in this section is in scope for the current phase. Each shall be assessed against the operating reality of the site before it is committed.
